A new approach to the 3-momentum regularization of the in-medium one and two fermion line integrals with applications to cross sections in the Nambu–Jona-Lasinio model
Renan Câmara Pereira, João Moreira, Pedro Costa, Constança Providência

Abstract
We propose the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization applied to the one and two fermion line integrals at finite temperature and chemical potential. The quark-antiquark polarization function in this new regularization approach is equivalent to the usual 3-momentum regularization, when the absolute value of the external 3-momentum of the polarization is zero. Additionally, it respects the particle-antiparticle symmetry of meson states in the Nambu − - Jona-Lasinio (NJL) model for all values of temperature and chemical potential. Without this symmetry, in-medium cross sections calculated in the 3-momentum regularized NJL model are not consistent. In order to demonstrate the difference between the usual 3-momentum regularization with the one proposed in this work, we study the quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections in both regularization schemes. To this end we use the standard S ​ U ​ ( 3 ) SU(3) NJL model, with four and six quark interactions. We observe major quantitative and qualitative differences when comparing quark-quark cross sections in both schemes. The quark-antiquark cross sections, on the other hand, are very similar in both regularizations, owning to the equivalence between the regularizations when the absolute value of the external 3-momentum is zero.



I
Introduction

The calculation of one loop integrals, at finite temperature and chemical potential, is an essential part of the study of in-medium quantum field theory. These integrals arise naturally in calculations performed under the framework of effective theories of quantum chromodynamics (QCD), like Nambu
-
Jona-Lasinio (NJL) type models
Rehberg et al. 1996a
;
Costa et al. 2004
;
Costa et al. 2005
;
Nagahiro et al. 2006
;
Costa et al. 2009
;
Contrera et al. 2010
;
Inagaki et al. 2011
;
Blanquier 2014
;
Blaschke et al. 2017
;
Costa and Pereira 2019
. In the NJL model, the one and two fermion line integrals occupy a central role in the calculation of meson propagators, meson masses, cross sections, or thermodynamic quantities, like pressure, energy density and entropy density. Moreover, quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections, derived from the NJL model, can be used to estimate the quark relaxation times, a vital part of several theoretical predictions about the in-medium behavior of transport coefficients like the shear and bulk viscosities, as well as, the electrical and thermal conductivities of strongly interacting matter
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
;
Zhuang et al. 1995
;
Sasaki and Redlich 2010
;
Marty et al. 2013
;
Soloveva et al. 2021
.

Due to the non-perturbative characteristics of QCD at low energies, phenomenological models, like the NJL model, become an essential tool to make qualitative predictions about QCD at finite temperature and density, specially when the goal is to study the behavior of the quark gluon plasma at temperatures and chemical potentials for which the
ab initio
approaches of QCD, like lattice gauge theory or perturbative based calculations, are not applicable
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Buballa 2005
. Hence, making sure that the calculation of one loop integrals, within the scope of NJL-type models, is made in a symmetry preserving manner, is extremely important in order to make better qualitative and quantitative predictions while also assuring that one is not introducing non-physical features in the phenomenological calculation.

In this work we follow the terminology given in Ref.
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
, and denote the one fermion line integral by
A
and the two fermion line integral by
B_{0}
. Here, we will not study the practical implementation of integrals with a fermion line counting higher then two. As it is well known, both of these integrals are divergent and some regularization technique must be employed, becoming an essential part of the calculations
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Ripka
. When studying finite temperature and chemical potential systems, the 3-momentum regularization is widely applied
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
. In this framework the integration over the internal 3-momentum of quarks,
\bm{p}
, is carried out up to a maximum value of its absolute value,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}<\Lambda
, with
\Lambda
the 3-momentum cutoff of the model
Ripka
. The usual way in which the 3-momentum regularization is performed breaks a symmetry of the system: the quark-antiquark polarization loop, which mixes light and strange quarks, is not symmetric with respect to the interchange of quarks
Rehberg et al. 1996b
. This originates from a shift of variables used in order to simplify the evaluation of the
B_{0}
integral
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
.

One of the main issues with this feature is that physical scattering processes which ought to be identical for all values of temperature and chemical potential, become different. A clear example is the cross sections of the quark-quark processes,
us\to us
and
su\to su
(the same occurs for
ds\to ds
and
sd\to sd
). Considering the
us\to us
scattering in the
u
-channel, the exchanged mesons are the pseudoscalar
K^{+}
and the scalar
\kappa^{+}
while, in the
t
-channel, the exchanged mesons are the flavorless, neutral mesons
\eta
,
\eta^{\prime}
,
\pi_{0}
,
\sigma
,
f_{0}
and
a_{0}^{0}
. For the
su\to su
process the corresponding antimesons are involved in each channel. Using a theoretical setup in which the quark-quark processes,
us\to us
and
su\to su
, yield different results is an indication that the set of meson propagators used in the calculations do not display the expected particle-antiparticle symmetry of meson states. One way to solve this problem is to follow the recipe introduced in Ref.
Rehberg et al. 1996b
and, when appropriate, replace the two fermion line integral present in the quark-antiquark polarization loop function,
B_{0}=B_{0}\quantity[M_u,M_s,T,\mu_u,\mu_s, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]
, with an average,
B_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( B_0 \qty[M_u,M_s,T,\mu_u,\mu_s, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}] + B_0 \qty[M_s,M_u,T,\mu_s,\mu_u, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}] )
. Here
M_{f}
and
\mu_{f}
are the effective mass and effective chemical potential of the quark of flavor
f
,
T
is the temperature and
k_{0}
,
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }
refer to the temporal-like and spacial-like parts of the external momentum, respectively. In the physical scenario where isospin symmetry does not occur (up and down quarks have different bare quark masses) or if there is an asymmetry caused by non-degenerate up and down quark chemical potentials (for example, when considering matter in beta equilibrium), several other scattering processes are also affected, such as the
ud\to ud
and
du\to du
processes.

In this work we propose a novel approach to the 3-momentum regularization, namely the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme. In this new regularization scheme, we regularize the one loop integrals based on the number of quark propagators present in the integrand. Each quark propagator has a 3-momentum which must be smaller than the 3-momentum cutoff,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}<\Lambda
. In the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization, the individual momentum of each quark propagators must be smaller than the cutoff, even when external momenta are considered. As we will discuss in this paper, this can be achieved by considering that the integration over the momentum in the
N
fermion line integrals must be evaluated inside the region defined by the intersection of
N
spheres. Furthermore, in the case in which there are no external momenta applied to the
N
fermion line integrals, the spheres completely intersect and the regularization is equivalent to the usual 3-momentum regularization. Interestingly, if the spheres do not intersect, the integral is automatically zero. As we will discuss, this happens for the meson propagators: if the momentum of the meson is larger than
2\Lambda
, the spheres corresponding to each quark propagator, inside the quark-antiquark polarization function, no longer intersect and the polarization function is automatically zero. With this approach to the 3-momentum regularization, all the symmetries of the system are maintained and the previously discussed problems with the scattering processes do not occur.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
II
, we derive the one and two fermion line integrals from the NJL model for any number of scalar and pseudoscalar quark-quark interactions. Such is accomplished in the mean field approximation by writing the linear and quadratic expansions of the NJL lagrangian. We also show how the expansion used to derive the meson propagators from the quadratic NJL model yields the same meson propagators as the ones obtained via the commonly employed Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation. Additionally, the 3-momentum sphere intersection is introduced and discussed. The formalism developed here can be used to incorporate multi-quark interactions, such as the eight quark proposed in
Osipov et al. 2006
;
Osipov et al. 2007
, or explicitly chiral symmetry breaking interactions, as those in
Osipov et al. 2013a
;
Osipov et al. 2013b
. In Sections
III
and
IV
, we evaluate the two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
, and the one fermion line integral,
A
, within the new regularization scheme. In Section
V
the formalism to evaluate quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections is laid out and a specific version of the
SU(3)
NJL model, with four and six quark interactions is introduced in order to study the effect of using both regularizations on the different scattering processes. The numerical results regarding quark-quark and quark-antiquark scattering processes for different scenarios of temperature and chemical potential are presented and discussed. Finally, in Section
VI
, conclusions are formulated and additional research is suggested.

II
The one and two fermion line integrals from NJL-type models

The one fermion line integral,
A
, and the two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
, at one loop level, arise when studying NJL-type models. In this section we show one way to derive the definitions of these integrals from the NJL model and discuss regularization strategies, namely a new way to regularize the quark-antiquark polarization function.

Consider a general model describing
N_{f}
flavors of fermions including different types of scalar and pseudoscalar interactions and the standard free Dirac Lagrangian. This can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\overline{\psi},\psi]=\overline{\psi}\quantity( i\slashed{\partial} - m )\psi+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}\quantity[ s_a, p_a ].

(1)

Here,
m=\diag\quantity{ m_0, m_1, \ldots, m_{N_f-1} }
is the quark current mass matrix, the term
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}
contains several types of quark-quark interactions and is written as a function of quark bilinear operators,
s_{a}=\overline{\psi}\lambda_{a}\psi
and
p_{a}=\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}\psi
, where
\lambda_{a}
are the
N_{f}^{2}
matrices that span the U
(N_{f})
algebra. The usual 4-quark scalar-pseudoscalar interaction, for example, can be written as,
\mathcal{L}_{4q}\propto(\overline{\psi}\lambda_{a}\psi)^{2}+(\overline{\psi}i\gamma_{5}\lambda_{a}\psi)^{2}=s_{a}^{2}+p_{a}^{2}
(Einstein summation convention is used).

Using this Lagrangian density, one can obtain the generating functional of the theory and derive all quantities of interest like the quark propagator, the thermodynamic potential and (anti)quark-(anti)quark cross sections (here and henceforth we are referring to quark-quark, quark-antiquark and antiquark-antiquark processes). However, the presence of more than two quark-quark interactions at the Lagrangian level renders impossible the exact integration over the quark fields
Reinhardt and Alkofer 1988
. This is a natural difficulty when dealing with interacting field theories and some approximation must be employed in order to deal with the functional integrations and calculate the previously mentioned quantities.

In this work, we will use the linear and quadratic expansions of the Lagrangian, allied to the mean field approximation. The one fermion line integral,
A
, arises in the linear expansion of the NJL Lagrangian, namely in the calculation of the quark propagator and thermodynamic quantities. The two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
, emerges in the quadratic expansion and is essential in the calculation of the quark-antiquark polarization function, which is a fundamental step in the calculation of meson mass behavior and the cross sections. In the following we show how these integrals arise from the linear and quadratic expansions of the Lagrangian defined in Eq. (
1
).

II.1
The linear Lagrangian

As already discussed, in order to obtain the quark propagator and thermodynamics of the model defined in Eq. (
1
), one can consider the linear expansion of the Lagrangian, allied to the mean field approximation. In this approximation one transforms all quark-quark interactions into quadratic quark interactions (linear in bilinear quark operators) by the introduction of auxiliary fields. Terms which have a larger than linear dependency in the quantum fluctuations are neglected. Using this technique, one can write a generating functional integrand that is quadratic in the quark fields.

Consider a quark bilinear operator,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}=\overline{\psi}\hat{\Gamma}\psi
, where
\hat{\Gamma}
is an operator that acts on some internal quark index, like color or flavor. Without loss of generality, the bilinear can be written as a sum between its mean field value and a small fluctuation,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}=\expectationvalue*{ \operator}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}
. In order to obtain the linear expansion of the product between
N-
bilinear operators, one only keeps terms that are linear in the fluctuations,
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}
, i.e., terms equal or superior to
(\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}})^{2}
are neglected. As a matter of fact, one can prove that the linear product between
N=n+1
commuting operators can be written using the following formula (see Appendix
A
for the proof)
Câmara Pereira et al. 2020
;
Pereira 2021
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1} \frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
- n
)\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }.

(2)

As an example, the linear product between two and three commuting operators, can directly be obtained from this equation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2},

(3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}.

(4)

Finally, using this approximation, one can obtain a Lagrangian density,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{L}}
, that is linear in the quark bilinear operators,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{L}}\quantity[\bar{\psi},\psi, \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}]=\overline{\psi}\quantity( i\slashed{\partial} - M \qty[m,\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}] )\psi-\mathcal{U}\quantity[ \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ].

(5)

Here,
\expectationvalue*{ \vec{s} }=\{\expectationvalue*{ s_0 },\ldots,\expectationvalue*{ s_{N_f^2-1} }\}
and
\expectationvalue*{ \vec{p} }=\{\expectationvalue*{ p_0 },\ldots,\expectationvalue*{ p_{N_f^2-1}}\}
are the sets of scalar and pseudoscalar mean fields obtained after performing the linearization procedure. These fields correspond to the expectation values of the
N_{f}^{2}
scalar and pseudoscalar quark bilinear operators,
s_{a}=\overline{\psi}\lambda_{a}\psi
and
p_{a}=\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}\psi
, respectively (
a=0,\ldots,N_{f}^{2}-1
). The effective mass matrix,
M
and the mean field potential
U
are functions of these sets. Using this quantity, one can find the generating functional of the model, within this approximation, to be given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{L}}\quantity[\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}},\bar{\eta},\eta]

\displaystyle\propto\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi\exp\quantity[
i \int\dd[4]{x}
\qty(
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{L}}
\qty[\bar{\psi},\psi,\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}]
+ \bar{\psi}\eta+ \bar{\eta}\psi)
]

\displaystyle\propto\exp\quantity[
-i \mathcal{U} \, V
]\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi\exp\quantity[
i \int\dd[4]{x}
\qty(
\int\dd[4]{y}
\bar{\psi} [x]
\,
\mathcal{G}_q^{-1} \qty[x,y]
\,
\psi[y]
+ \bar{\psi} [x] \eta+ \bar{\eta}\psi[x] )
],

(6)

where
\eta
and
\overline{\eta}
are the quark and antiquark sources,
V
is the four-dimensional volume and the inverse quark propagator,
\mathcal{G}_{q}^{-1}
, was defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{q}^{-1}\quantity[x,y]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
i\slashed{\partial} - M
)\delta^{4}\quantity(x-y).

(7)

For simplicity, we omitted the mean field dependencies present on the quark effective mass,
M=M[m,\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
.

The quark fields can then be integrated out, and one can define the effective action of the model from the generating functional (with vanishing sources),
\Gamma\quantity[ \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]=-i\ln\quantity[ \mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{L}}
\qty[ \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ] ]
, which only depends on the sets of mean fields,
\expectationvalue{\vec{s}}
and
\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}
. Such dependence must be removed by requiring the effective action to be an extreme with respect to these mean fields i.e.,
\functionalderivative*{ \Gamma\qty[\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}] }{\expval{s_a}}=\functionalderivative*{ \Gamma\qty[\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}] }{\expval{p_a}}=0
. At this point one has access to the thermodynamic potential from which several thermodynamic variables of interest, such as pressure, energy density, entropy and speed of sound, can be evaluated.

Finally, the one fermion, one loop integral,
A
, can be defined by considering the quark condensate of flavor
i

Klevansky 1992
:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_i \psi_i }

\displaystyle=-i\tr\mathcal{G}_{q}^{i}\quantity[x,x]

\displaystyle=-4iN_{c}M_{i}\int\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p^{2}-M_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{c}}{4\pi^{2}}M_{i}A\quantity[M_i].

(8)

The one fermion, one loop integral in Minkowski spacetime,
A\quantity[M_f]
, is given by:

\displaystyle A\quantity[M_i]=-16\pi^{2}i\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{p^{2}-M_{i}^{2}}.

(9)

Here,
M_{i}
is the
i
-quark effective mass. The inclusion of temperature and chemical potential will be done in Section
IV
. Since this integral is divergent and some regularization scheme must be applied, we explicitly wrote the label
\mathrm{Reg}
.

II.2
The quadratic Lagrangian

The two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
, can be found when considering the quadratic expansion of the Lagrangian given in Eq. (
1
). Such expansion allows one to calculate several interesting properties, namely: meson propagators, meson masses, decay rates and (anti)quark-(anti)quark cross sections.

The approach to write a Lagrangian that is quadratic in the quark bilinear operators (fourth order in the quark fields) is similar to the one used in the previous section to obtain the linear Lagrangian. Firstly, as before, the operator is written as the sum,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}=\expectationvalue*{ \operator}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}
. However, to get the quadratic expansion of the product between
N
-bilinear operators, only up to quadratic terms in the fluctuations are kept and higher order terms are neglected. As before, instead of doing such expansion by hand, there is a formula from which one can readily obtain the quadratic product between
N=n+2
commuting operators, with
n\geq 1
(see Appendix
B
for the proof). It is given by
Câmara Pereira et al. 2020
;
Pereira 2021
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n+2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}\sum_{j=1}^{n+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )-n\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}+\frac{n}{2}\quantity(n+1)\bigg)\prod_{k=1}^{n+2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }.

(10)

As an example, the quadratic expansion of the product between three and four operators, are given by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]=

\displaystyle-\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}-\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}-\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3},

(11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]=

\displaystyle-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle+3\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}.

(12)

Using the quadratic expansion above and neglecting second order terms which mix scalar and pseudoscalar quark bilinear operators (
s_{a}p_{b}
and
p_{a}s_{b}
)
1
1

1

This follows from the consideration that only the scalar condensates get a non-vanishing ground state and pseudoscalar condensates are zero.
, the quadratic Lagrangian density,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Q}}
, (fourth order in the quark fields) can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Q}}\quantity[\bar{\psi},\psi,\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}]=\overline{\psi}\quantity(i\slashed{\partial} - m )\psi+s_{a}S_{ab}s_{b}+p_{a}P_{ab}p_{b}+s_{a}S_{a}+p_{a}P_{a}+R.

(13)

Here,
S_{ab}=S_{ab}[\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
and
P_{ab}=P_{ab}[\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
gather the coefficients that arise after the quadratic expansion of the operators, which are quadratic in the variables
s_{a}=\overline{\psi}\lambda_{a}\psi
and
p_{a}=\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}\psi
, respectively. They are the so-called meson projectors for the scalar modes
(S_{ab})
and the pseudoscalar modes
(P_{ab})
. The first and zeroth order contributions are gathered in the coefficients
S_{a}=S_{a}[\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
,
P_{a}=P_{a}[\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
and
R=R[\expectationvalue{\vec{s}},\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}]
, respectively.

The generating functional for the system, can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{Q}}\quantity[ \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} , \eta, \bar{\eta} ]\propto\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi\exp\quantity{
i \int\dd[4]{x}
\qty(
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Q}}
\qty[\bar{\psi},\psi,\expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}}]
+ \overline{\psi} \eta+ \bar{\eta} \psi)
}.

(14)

In order to integrate out the quark fields, one can bosonize the model by introducing auxiliary meson fields,
\sigma_{a}
and
\pi_{a}
, through Hubbard–Stratonovich transformations
Hubbard 1959
;
Stratonovich 1957
;
Ebert 1998
. For the
s_{a}
contributions, consider the following Gaussian integral:

\displaystyle\exp\quantity{
i \int\dd[4]{x} \qty( s_a S_{ab} s_b )
}

\displaystyle\propto\int\prod_{c}\mathcal{D}\sigma_{c}\exp\quantity{ i \int\dd[4]{x}
\qty(
- \frac{1}{4} \sigma_a \qty( S ^{-1})_{ab} \sigma_b
-s_a \sigma_a
)
}.

(15)

Considering a change of variables on the functional integral as:
\sigma_{a}\to\sigma_{a}+\quantity(S_a + \Delta_a)
where
\Delta_{a}=M_{a}-m_{a}
i.e.,
\lambda_{a}\Delta_{a}=M-m
, and multiplying both sides by
\exp\quantity{
i \int\dd[4]{x} \qty( s_a S_a )
}
, we can write
2
2

2

Here we used the fact that the matrix
S
is symmetric.
:

\displaystyle\exp\{i\int\differential[4]{x}\quantity( s_a S_{ab} s_b + s_a S_a )\bigg\}

\displaystyle\propto\int\prod_{c}\mathcal{D}\sigma_{c}\exp\{

\displaystyle i\int\differential[4]{x}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{a}\quantity( S ^{-1})_{ab}\sigma_{b}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{a}\quantity( S^{-1})_{ab}\quantity(S_b + \Delta_b)-s_{a}\quantity( \sigma_a + \Delta_a )\bigg)\bigg\}.

(16)

With this particular change of variables we are considering the possibility of scalar condensation in the vacuum. In this work we do not consider the possibility of pseudoscalar condensation. In this case, for the
p_{a}
contributions, the approach is similar except that the change of variables is simply
\pi_{a}\to\pi_{a}+P_{a}
. Using these identities, the action becomes quadratic in the fermion fields and one can integrate out the quark fields to yield:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{Q}}\quantity[ \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]

\displaystyle\propto\int\prod_{a}\mathcal{D}\sigma_{a}\mathcal{D}\pi_{a}\exp\quantity{
i \mathcal{S}_\mathrm{Q}
\qty[ \vec{\sigma}, \vec{\pi} , \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]
}.

(17)

Here,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Q}}\quantity[ \vec{\sigma}, \vec{\pi} , \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]
is the action of the system and
\bm{\sigma}=\{\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{N_{f}^{2}-1}\}
and
\bm{\pi}=\{\pi_{0},\ldots,\pi_{N_{f}^{2}-1}\}
are the sets of scalar and pseudoscalar auxiliary fields (respectively) introduced with the Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation. The action of the system is explicitly given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Q}}\quantity[ \vec{\sigma}, \vec{\pi}, \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]=-i\Tr\ln[i\not{\partial}-M

\displaystyle-\lambda_{a}\sigma_{a}-i\gamma_{5}\lambda_{a}\pi_{a}\big]

\displaystyle+\int\differential[4]{x}\bigg(

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{a}\quantity( S^{-1})_{ab}\sigma_{b}-\frac{1}{4}\pi_{a}\quantity( P^{-1} )_{ab}\pi_{b}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{a}\quantity( S^{-1})_{ab}\quantity(S_b + \Delta_b)-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{a}\quantity( P^{-1} )_{ab}P_{b}+R\bigg).

(18)

In the above expressions,
\Tr
is the usual notation for the total trace over continuous spacetime indexes and discrete internal indexes such as color, flavor and spinor,
\Tr\equiv\trx\trc\trf\trD
and the functional trace of an operator,
\hat{O}
, is defined by:
\trx\hat{O}=\int\differential[d]{x}\bra{ x }\hat{O}\ket{ x }

Ball 1989
;
Klevansky 1992
. Here, we will use the functional formalism introduced in Refs.
Novikov et al. 1984
;
Fraser 1985
;
Aitchison and Fraser 1985
, where
\hat{O}
is considered an operator acting directly on an fictitious Hilbert space,
\quantity{ \ket{x} }
, with the position (
\hat{x}_{\mu}
) and momentum operators (
\hat{p}_{\mu}
) obeying the usual commutation relation,
\quantity[\hat{x}_\mu,\hat{p}_\nu]=i\delta_{\mu\nu}

Novikov et al. 1984
;
Fraser 1985
;
Aitchison and Fraser 1985
;
Ball 1989
.

The gap equations of the model can be obtained by evaluating the path integral over the auxiliary meson fields,
\sigma_{a}
and
\pi_{a}
in the stationary phase (or saddle point) approximation
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Ebert 1998
;
Ripka
;
Oertel et al. 2001
, which is equivalent to the mean field or Hartree approximation. It amounts to requiring the action of the model to be stationary with respect to the auxiliary meson fields,

\left.\functionalderivative*{ \mathcal{S}_\mathrm{Q} }{ \sigma_a}\right|_{\quantity{\sigma^{\mathrm{st}}_a,\pi^{\mathrm{st}}_a}}=\left.\functionalderivative*{ \mathcal{S}_\mathrm{Q} }{ \pi_a }\right|_{\quantity{\sigma^{\mathrm{st}}_a,\pi^{\mathrm{st}}_a}}=0
. Here, we note that we already allowed for a non-vanishing expectation value of the field
\sigma_{a}
in the change of variables,
\sigma_{a}\to\sigma_{a}+\quantity(S_a + \Delta_a)
, and thus are requiring that the stationary field configurations must vanish:
\sigma^{\mathrm{st}}_{a}=\pi^{\mathrm{st}}_{a}=0
. Using these requirements, one can derive the self-consistent gap equations of the model which relates the quarks effective mass,
M
, and the expectation values of the quark bilinear operators,
\expectationvalue{\vec{s}}
and
\expectationvalue{\vec{p}}
. This approach leads exactly to the same gap equations as the ones obtained using the linear Lagrangian in the mean field approximation presented in the previous section. Indeed, for a specific NJL Lagrangian, one can check this equivalence explicitly. This correspondence might be related the exact Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation, used to transform the quadratic action (in terms of quark bilinear operators) into a linear action. A proper demonstration, between the equivalence of the gap equations derived in the linear and quadratic Lagrangians (alongside the saddle point approximation for the auxiliary meson fields), for any interactions terms, is left as future work.

The quark-antiquark polarization function, at finite chemical potential, can be derived from this formalism by considering the expansion of the fermionic determinant to second order in the mesonic auxiliary fields,
\sigma_{a}
and
\pi_{a}
. Finite chemical potential can be included in the calculation by making the following substitution,
\partial_{\alpha}\to D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}-i\mu\delta_{\alpha 0}
, with
\mu
the quark chemical potential matrix in flavor space
Klevansky 1992
. This matrix can be considered to be diagonal with components,
\mu=\diag\quantity( \mu_0, \mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{N_f-1})
. Next, we consider the Taylor series expansion of the logarithm of a matrix in Eq. (
18
). We write
Klevansky 1992
;
Nikolov et al. 1996
;
Costa et al. 2003
;
Costa et al. 2004
;
Blaschke et al. 2014
;
Blaschke and Ebert 2017
;
Haber 2023
:

\displaystyle\Tr\ln[i\not{D}-M-\lambda_{a}\sigma_{a}-i\gamma_{5}\lambda_{a}\pi_{a}\big]

\displaystyle=\Tr\ln[i\not{D}-M\big]+\Tr\ln\quantity[
1
+
\qty[i\slashed{D} - M]^{-1}
\bigg(- \lambda_a\sigma_a - i\gamma_5 \lambda_a \pi_a \bigg)
]

\displaystyle=\Tr\ln[i\not{D}-M\big]-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Tr\quantity[ T_n ].

(19)

In the above, the logarithm of a matrix was written using its defining Taylor series expansion (assuming that the series converges)
Haber 2023
:

\displaystyle\ln\quantity[X]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\quantity(-1)^{n+1}}{n}\quantity( X - I )^{n}.

(20)

A convergence criterion can be written as:
\absolutevalue{ X - I }<1
, with
\absolutevalue{Y}
a proper way to evaluate the norm of the matrix
Y

Haber 2023
. Additionally, the operator
T_{n}
is defined as:

\displaystyle T_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\quantity(
\qty[i\slashed{D} - M]^{-1}
\qty(\lambda_a\sigma_a + i\gamma_5 \lambda_a \pi_a )
)^{n}.

(21)

We are interested in evaluating the second order term in the expansion,
T_{2}
, which is an essential part in the definition of the inverse meson propagators. To this end, we define the operators,
\mathcal{G}=\quantity[i\slashed{D} - M]^{-1}
,
\sigma=\lambda_{a}\sigma_{a}
and
\pi=\lambda_{a}\pi_{a}
. We highlight that
\mathcal{G}
is a matrix in flavor space due to the structure of the effective quark mass and effective chemical potential matrices,
M=\diag\quantity( M_0, M_1, \dots, M_{N_f-1} )
, and
\mu=\diag\quantity( \mu_0, \mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{N_f-1})
. With this notation, we can write:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{G}\quantity(\sigma+ i\gamma_5 \pi)\mathcal{G}\quantity(\sigma+ i\gamma_5 \pi)

\displaystyle=T_{2}^{\sigma\sigma}+T_{2}^{\sigma\pi}+T_{2}^{\pi\sigma}+T_{2}^{\pi\pi}.

(22)

Only the traces of the first and fourth operators are nonzero,
T_{2}^{\sigma\sigma}
and
T_{2}^{\pi\pi}
. The trace over spinor indexes of the “mixed” contributions involving the identity matrix
I_{4}
and the
\gamma^{5}
matrix vanish. Without loss of generality, we can deal with the quantity,
T_{2}^{\phi\phi}\quantity[ \Gamma_1, \Gamma_2 ]=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{G}\Gamma_{1}\phi\mathcal{G}\Gamma_{2}\phi
, with
\phi
standing in for
\sigma
or
\pi
. It yields
T_{2}^{\sigma\sigma}
when
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=I_{4}
(
I_{4}
is the four-dimensional unity matrix) and
T_{2}^{\pi\pi}
in the case in which
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=i\gamma_{5}
. The operator
\Gamma
only has discrete Dirac indexes. The fields
\phi
and the quantity
\mathcal{G}
both have discrete flavor indexes as well as continuous spacetime indexes but are structureless in color space. Additionally, the operator
\mathcal{G}
also carries Dirac indexes. As a result of this internal structure, the evaluation of the traces over these different vector spaces has to be done taking into account the noncommutative nature of these operators, i.e., one has to take into account that,
\quantity[\mathcal{G},\Gamma]\neq 0
in spinor space,
\quantity[\mathcal{G},\phi]\neq 0
both for flavor and continuous spacetime indexes, while the remaining commutation relations are zero (for instance,
\quantity[\Gamma,\phi]=0
in flavor and spinor spaces). The way in which one evaluates the traces can lead to different mathematical expansions. As we will explain more thoroughly later, we are interested in obtaining an expansion of the fermionic determinant which yields the same result for the meson propagators as the ones obtained via Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation (or, equivalently, the ring approximation
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
). We will make further comments about deriving the meson propagators from the fermionic determinant in Section
II.2.2
.

Lets start by evaluating the trace over flavor degrees of freedom,
\trf\quantity[ \mathcal{G} \Gamma_1 \phi\mathcal{G} \Gamma_2 \phi]
. To this end we introduce the matrices
\hat{e}_{AB}
, with indexes in the range
A,B=\quantity{0,1,\ldots,N_f-1}
and matrix elements given by:
\quantity(\hat{e}_{AB})_{ij}=\delta_{Ai}\delta_{Bj}
. Explicitly, they are given by:

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{00}

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
1 & 0 & \ldots& 0 \\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0 \\
\vdots& \vdots& \ddots& \vdots\\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0
\endlx@physics@matrix),\;\hat{e}_{01}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
0 & 1 & \ldots& 0 \\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0 \\
\vdots& \vdots& \ddots& \vdots\\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0
\endlx@physics@matrix),\;\ldots,\;\hat{e}_{N_{f}-1N_{f}-1}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0 \\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 0 \\
\vdots& \vdots& \ddots& \vdots\\
0 & 0 & \ldots& 1
\endlx@physics@matrix).

(23)

Any
N_{f}\times N_{f}
complex matrix,
C
, can be written as a linear combination of the
\hat{e}_{AB}
matrices alongside
N_{f}^{2}
complex coefficients,
c_{AB}
, as:
C=c_{AB}\hat{e}_{AB}
. These matrices are linearly independent since the zero matrix can only be obtained if all coefficients vanish. Additionally, one can check that the trace between two
\hat{e}_{AB}
matrices is,
\tr\quantity[ \hat{e}_{AB} \hat{e}_{CD} ]=\delta_{AD}\delta_{BC}
. The
N_{f}^{2}
generators of the
U(N_{f})
group,
\lambda_{a}
, can be written in terms of this new basis. Using the completeness relation we can write:
\lambda_{a}=R_{AB}^{a}\hat{e}_{AB}
. Multiplying both sides by
\hat{e}_{CD}
and taking the trace, one can get the expression for the
R_{AB}^{a}
coefficients:

\displaystyle R_{AB}^{a}=\tr\quantity[ \lambda_a \hat{e}_{BA} ].

(24)

Using this new set of matrices, the trace over flavor space is:

\displaystyle\trf\quantity[
\mathcal{G} \Gamma_1 \phi\mathcal{G} \Gamma_2 \phi]

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{ii}\Gamma_{1}\phi_{a}\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{jj}\Gamma_{2}\phi_{b}.

(25)

Here,
a,b,i,j=\{0,1,\ldots,N_{f}-1\}
and the coefficients
R^{a}_{AB}
are given by Eq. (
24
). Also, we used the fact that the operator
\mathcal{G}
is diagonal in flavor space,
\mathcal{G}=\diag\quantity( \mathcal{G}_0, \mathcal{G}_1, \ldots, \mathcal{G}_{N_f-1} )
. For the
N_{f}=3
case, which will be studied later in this work, the nine generators of the
U(3)
group are constituted by the scaled unit matrix plus the eight Gell-Mann matrices and one can identify the
\quantity(\mathcal{G})_{ii}
elements with the up, down and strange quark propagators, i.e.,
i=0=u
(up),
i=1=d
(down) and
i=2=s
(strange) and
\mathcal{G}=\diag\quantity( \mathcal{G}_u, \mathcal{G}_d, \mathcal{G}_s )
.

Due to the lack of structure in color space, the trace over such degrees of freedom yields a simple
N_{c}
factor. The full trace over spacetime and discrete indexes is given by:

\displaystyle\Tr\quantity[ T_2^{ \phi\phi} \qty[ \Gamma_1, \Gamma_2 ] ]

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\frac{N_{c}}{2}\trD\int\differential[4]{x}\bra{ x }\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{ii}\Gamma_{1}\phi_{a}\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{jj}\Gamma_{2}\phi_{b}\ket{ x }.

(26)

Evaluating it, yields the following result:

\displaystyle\Tr\quantity[ T_2^{ \phi\phi} \qty[ \Gamma_1, \Gamma_2 ] ]

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\frac{N_{c}}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\phi_{a}\quantity[ k ]\phi_{b}\quantity[ - k ]\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[k]}\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\trD\quantity[
\frac{1}{ \slashed{p}_i - M_i }
\Gamma_1
\frac{1}{ (\slashed{p}_j - \slashed{k}) - M_j }
\Gamma_2
]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\phi_{a}\quantity[ k ]\phi_{b}\quantity[ - k ]\Pi_{ij}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\phi_{a}\quantity[ k ]\,\Pi_{ab}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]\,\phi_{b}\quantity[ - k ].

(27)

Here, we defined
\not{p}_{f}=\gamma^{0}(p_{0}+\mu_{f})-\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{p}
, with
\mu_{f}
the chemical potential of the quark with flavor
f

Klevansky 1992
. Furthermore, we applied regularization to both momentum integrations, with a small caveat: we allowed the regularization of the innermost momentum integration (
p
) to be a function of the outermost momentum (
k
). The reason for this will be discussed later. The quark-antiquark polarization function with flavor indexes,
\Pi_{ij}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]
and its analogue,
\Pi_{ab}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]
, with indexes coming from the generators of the
U(N_{f})
group, were defined in this expression as:

\displaystyle\Pi_{ij}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]

\displaystyle=iN_{c}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[k]}\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\trD\quantity[ ( \slashed{p}_i + M_i ) \Gamma_1
((\slashed{p}_j - \slashed{k}) + M_j) \Gamma_2 ]}{(p_{i}^{2}-M_{i}^{2})((p_{j}-k)^{2}-M_{j}^{2})},

(28)

\displaystyle\Pi_{ab}\quantity[\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2, k]

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\Pi_{ij}\quantity[k].

(29)

The only trace left to calculate is over Dirac indexes. This can be achieved by choosing
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. As previously stated, only the cases
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=I_{4}
and
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=i\gamma^{5}
are nonzero. Thus, we define the scalar quark-antiquark polarization function as
\Pi_{ij}^{S}[M_{i},M_{j},\mu_{i},\mu_{j},k]=\Pi_{ij}\quantity[I_4, I_4, k]
, and the pseudoscalar quark-antiquark polarization function by
\Pi_{ij}^{P}[M_{i},M_{j},\mu_{i},\mu_{j},k]=\Pi_{ij}\quantity[i\gamma^5, i\gamma^5, k]
. Evaluating the trace over spinor space yields:

\displaystyle\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}[M_{i},M_{j},\mu_{i},\mu_{j},k]=-\frac{N_{c}}{8\pi^{2}}\bigg\{\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle[M_{i},\mu_{i},k]+\mathcal{A}[M_{j},\mu_{j},k]

\displaystyle+\quantity( \qty( M_i \pm M_j )^2 - \qty( \mu_i - \mu_j + k_0 )^2 + \vec{k}^2 )B_{0}[M_{i},M_{j},\mu_{i},\mu_{j},k]\bigg\}.

(30)

In the right-hand side of this equation, the positive sign
(+)
is used for the scalar function,
\Pi_{ij}^{S}
, while, the negative sign
(-)
, is used for the pseudoscalar quark-antiquark polarization function,
\Pi_{ij}^{P}
. Here, we also defined the two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
, in Minkowski spacetime:

\displaystyle B_{0}[M_{i},M_{j},\mu_{i},\mu_{j},k]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}i\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[k]}\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{(\quantity(p_0 + \mu_i)^{2}-\bm{p}^{2}-M_{i}^{2})((p_{0}+\mu_{j}-k_{0})^{2}-(\bm{p}-\bm{k})^{2}-M_{j}^{2})}.

(31)

The function
\mathcal{A}
is defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},\mu_{i},k]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}i\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[k]}\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{\quantity(p_0 + \mu_i)^{2}-\bm{p}^{2}-M_{i}^{2}}.

(32)

The integrand is exactly the same as the one present in the definition of the one fermion line integral
A
(see Eq. (
9
)). However, the function
\mathcal{A}
carries the same momentum dependent regularization as the integral
B_{0}
. In the special case where the same regularization is applied to both
A
and
\mathcal{A}
, then
A=\mathcal{A}
. Hence, the integral
A
, defined in Eq. (
9
) and the integral
\mathcal{A}
, defined above, are exactly the same quantity but, within different regularizations.

Finally, we can define the meson propagators of the model. Using the series expansion of Eq. (
19
) alongside the trace calculation of Eq. (
27
). The second order term, proportional to
\sigma_{a}\sigma_{b}
and
\pi_{a}\pi_{b}
, of the quadratic action,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Q}}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Q}}^{(2)}\quantity[ \vec{\sigma}, \vec{\pi}, \expval{\vec{s}}, \expval{\vec{p}} ]=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\,\sigma_{a}\quantity[ k ]\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
\qty( S^{-1})_{ab}
-
\Pi_{ab}^S \qty[k]
)\sigma_{b}\quantity[ - k ]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\,\pi_{a}\quantity[ k ]\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
\qty( P^{-1})_{ab}
-
\Pi_{ab}^P \qty[k]
)\pi_{b}\quantity[ - k ].

(33)

Using this result we can define the inverse scalar and pseudoscalar meson propagators,
D_{S}^{-1}
and
D_{P}^{-1}
, respectively. They are defined by:

\displaystyle\quantity( D_S^{-1} )_{ab}\quantity[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( S^{-1})_{ab}-\Pi_{ab}^{S}\quantity[k],

(34)

\displaystyle\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{ab}\quantity[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( P^{-1} )_{ab}-\Pi_{ab}^{P}\quantity[k].

(35)

As we will see later, the meson propagators are essential in the calculation of quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections.

II.2.1
Equivalence with the Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation

Figure 1
:
Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation (RPA)
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Oertel et al. 2001
;
Buballa 2005
. In the first line the transfer matrix,
\mathcal{T}
, is written as an infinite sum over quark-antiquark polarization loops,
\Pi
. In the second line, the equation is expressed in a self-consistent way. The double lines represents the meson propagator and the solid lines the quark propagators. The wiggly line between two black dots is used to signal the tensor nature of the NJL scattering kernel,
\mathcal{K}
, due to its structure in flavor, color and Dirac spaces.

Another commonly employed method to obtain the meson propagators involves the Bethe-Salpeter equation, which describes the bound states of two body quantum systems. In the so-called random phase approximation, this equation is given by (see Fig.
1
for its diagrammatic representation)
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
:

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}\quantity[k]

\displaystyle=i\mathcal{K}+i\mathcal{K}\Big(-i\Pi\quantity[k]\Big)i\mathcal{K}+i\mathcal{K}\Big(-i\Pi\quantity[k]\Big)i\mathcal{K}\Big(-i\Pi\quantity[k]\Big)i\mathcal{K}+\ldots

\displaystyle=i\mathcal{K}+i\mathcal{K}\Big(-i\Pi\quantity[k]\Big)i\mathcal{T}\quantity[k].

(36)

Here,
\mathcal{T}
is the so-called quark-antiquark scattering matrix,
\mathcal{K}
is the scattering kernel and
\Pi\quantity[k]
is the quark-antiquark polarization loop (see Fig.
2
). These quantities contain several indexes including color, flavor and spinor.

Figure 2
:
Diagram representing the scalar/pseudoscalar quark-antiquark polarization function
Klevansky 1992
. The solid lines represent quark propagators and the wiggly line with a black dot represents
\Gamma_{a}^{X}
, with
\Gamma_{a}^{S}=\lambda_{a}
, for scalar modes and
\Gamma_{a}^{P}=i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}
, for pseudoscalar modes.

The Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random-phase approximation, applied to NJL-type models in the mean field approximation (or Hartree approximation
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Oertel et al. 2001
;
Buballa 2005
), has been extensively applied in the literature to study in-medium meson behavior, and to evaluate quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections
Bernard et al. 1988
;
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Rehberg et al. 1996a
;
Zhuang et al. 1995
;
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
;
Oertel et al. 2001
;
Buballa 2005
;
Saionji et al. 2023
. Such standard approach
Oertel et al. 2001
, is known to be a symmetry preserving one, being compatible with chiral symmetry and respecting the Goldstone theorem and the Gell-Mann
-
Oakes
-
Renner relation
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Nikolov et al. 1996
;
Oertel et al. 2001
;
Buballa 2005
. In this section we highlight the equivalence between this method and the functional expansion derived previously. To this end, one can use Eq. (
36
), to derive a self-consistent expression for the scalar and pseudoscalar meson propagator,
D_{ab}^{S|P}
. Consider the transfer matrix written in terms of the meson propagator:
\mathcal{T}=D^{XY}_{ab}\quantity( \Gamma_a^X \otimes\Gamma_b^Y )
, with
\Gamma_{a}^{X}
carrying the tensor structure of the vertex,
\Gamma_{a}^{S}=\lambda_{a}
and
\Gamma_{a}^{P}=i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}

Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Oertel et al. 2001
. In a theory with only four-Fermi interactions (like the one considered in this work, see the quadratic Lagrangian in Eq. (
13
)), the scattering kernel is given by:
\mathcal{K}=2g_{ab}^{XY}\quantity( \Gamma_a^X \otimes\Gamma_b^Y )
, with
g_{ab}^{XY}=g_{ab}^{X}\,\delta_{XY}
and
g_{ab}^{S}=S_{ab}
and
g_{ab}^{P}=P_{ab}
for scalar and pseudoscalar degrees of freedom, respectively
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Oertel et al. 2001
. The factor of
2
comes from the Feynman rules and is due to the application of Wick’s theorem
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
. The polarization loop,
\Pi_{ab}^{XY}
is the quark-antiquark polarization loop defined by
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Oertel et al. 2001
;
Buballa 2005
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{ab}^{XY}\quantity[k]

\displaystyle=i\int\frac{\differential[4]{p}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\tr\quantity[
\frac{ \slashed{p} + M }{ p^2 - M^2 }
\Gamma_a^X
\frac{ (\slashed{p} - \slashed{k}) + M }{ ({p} - {k})^2 - M^2 }
\Gamma_b^Y
].

(37)

Here, the trace is made over flavor, color and Dirac discrete indexes, i.e.,
\tr\equiv\trD\trc\trf
. After evaluating the trace, this expression agrees with the polarization loop obtained via functional methods, defined in Eqs. (
28
) and (
29
). A diagrammatic representation of the quark-antiquark polarization function is shown in Fig.
2
. Using these definitions, one can write an equation for the meson propagator as:

\displaystyle D_{ab}^{XY}\quantity( \Gamma_a^X \otimes\Gamma_b^Y )=2g_{ab}^{XY}\quantity( \Gamma_a^X \otimes\Gamma_b^Y )+2g_{ac}^{XK}\Pi_{cd}^{KL}D_{db}^{LY}\quantity( \Gamma_a^X \otimes\Gamma_b^Y ).

(38)

Since the matrix structure is completely contained in the
\Gamma_{a}^{X}
quantities, we can write an equation for the scalar function,
D_{ab}^{XY}
:

\displaystyle D_{ab}^{XY}=2g_{ab}^{XY}+2g_{ac}^{XK}\Pi_{cd}^{KL}D_{db}^{LY}.

(39)

Considering
D_{ab}^{XY}
as matrix elements, we can write the above equation in matrix form
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Saionji et al. 2023
,
D=2g+2g\Pi D
, which can be solved for the meson propagator,
D
, to yield
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Klevansky 1992
;
Buballa 2005
;
Saionji et al. 2023
:

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\quantity[ 1 - 2 g \Pi]^{-1}2g.

(40)

Writing the flavor indexes explicitly, we can write the scalar and pseudoscalar meson propagators,
D^{S|P}_{ab}
as:

\displaystyle D^{S|P}_{ab}=\quantity[ \frac{1}{2} \qty(S|P^{-1})_{ab} - \Pi^{S|P}_{ab} ]^{-1}.

(41)

Hence, the meson propagators obtained using the Bethe-Salpeter equation, in the random phase approximation, applied to the four-fermion Lagrangian density of Eq. (
13
), are identical to the ones obtained from the functional expansion presented earlier in this work, see Eqs. (
34
) and (
35
).

II.2.2
Comments on the expansion of the fermionic determinant

In the aforementioned calculations, the meson propagators were acquired through a specific expansion method applied to the fermionic determinant. Our approach involved utilizing the series representation of the logarithm of a matrix to derive the expansion. However, the expansion obtained is not unique and our particular choice is rooted on the fact that we wanted to derive the same expression for the meson propagators as the one obtained using the Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation
Klevansky 1992
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
;
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Hatsuda and Kunihiro 1994
;
Vogl and Weise 1991
;
Lutz et al. 1992
;
Oertel et al. 2001
.

In a different expansion, one could have introduced in the calculation kinetic terms for the meson fields. Such step could have been made when evaluating the functional trace over the continuous spacetime indexes. Since the operator
\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{jj}
and the fields
\phi_{a}
do not commute, instead of directly evaluating the trace in Eq. (
26
), one could evaluate the trace over these degrees of freedom independently, by moving all the functions of momentum operators to the left and all functions of the position operator to the right, as introduced in Refs.
Novikov et al. 1984
;
Fraser 1985
;
Aitchison and Fraser 1985
;
Ball 1989
. This can be accomplished by using the following identity
Fraser 1985
;
Aitchison and Fraser 1985
:

\displaystyle\phi\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}\phi+\mathcal{G}^{2}\quantity[ \mathcal{G}^{-1} , \phi]+\mathcal{G}^{3}\quantity[ \mathcal{G}^{-1} , \qty[ \mathcal{G}^{-1} , \phi] ]+\ldots

(42)

As a side note, the first term in this identity diverges, requiring regularization, while the following terms are not divergent
Ripka
. By substituting
\mathcal{G}=\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{jj}
and
\phi=\phi_{a}
one can understand that the tower of iterated commutators are proportional to field derivatives like
\partial_{\mu}\phi_{a}
,
p\cdot\partial\phi_{a}
,
\Box\phi_{a}
, etc. Following the use of this identity, one can write Eq. (
26
) as:

\displaystyle\Tr\quantity[ T_2^{ \phi\phi} \qty[ \Gamma_1, \Gamma_2 ] ]

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{a}R_{ji}^{b}\frac{N_{c}}{2}\trD\int\differential[4]{x}\bra{ x }\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{ii}\Gamma_{1}\quantity( \mathcal{G} )_{jj}\Gamma_{2}\ket{ x }\phi_{a}(x)\phi_{b}(x)+\text{$\phi$ derivative terms}.

(43)

Hence, in such expansion, the action of the model would contain kinetic terms for the meson fields,
\sigma_{a}
and
\pi_{a}
, not present in the our previous calculation. Additionally, only one fermion line quark loops would contribute to the calculation without any contribution coming from the two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
.

We would also like to highlight the fact that such result is only obtained if one starts the evaluation of the total trace with the trace over flavor space. Starting the trace evaluation by applying directly the identity of Eq. (
42
), as a first step in the trace calculation leads to an infinite tower of terms proportional to iterated commutators between the mass matrix
M
and the fields,
\quantity[M,\phi]
,
\quantity[M, \qty[M,\phi]]
, …Of course, in the case in which the mass matrix is diagonal and degenerate in flavor space,
M=M_{q}\diag\quantity( 1, 1, \dots, 1 )
, all these contribution vanish (with
M_{q}
the effective quark mass). However in the case in which this matrix is not diagonal and degenerate, this cancellation does not occur. This is the case when considering the physical scenario of
N_{f}=3
in which the strange quark mass is considerably higher than the up and down quark masses. Furthermore, when considering finite chemical potential, there is an extra matrix contribution to the
\mathcal{G}^{-1}
operator, in the form of an effective quark chemical potential matrix,
\mu=\diag\quantity( \mu_0, \mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{N_f-1} )
. Just like the mass case, in physical scenarios in which this quantity is not diagonal and degenerate an infinite sequence of iterated commutators between
\mu
and the fields appears,
\quantity[\mu,\phi]
,
\quantity[\mu, \qty[\mu,\phi]]
, …Finally, if one wishes to allow for the possibility of pseudoscalar condensation in the medium, the operator
\mathcal{G}^{-1}
will certainly contain contributions which are not diagonal and degenerate.

To end this section we would like to point out another technique that can be used to extract the meson propagators from the fermionic determinant, the so-called heat kernel expansion
Ball 1989
;
Ebert and Reinhardt 1986
;
Alkofer and Reinhardt 1995
;
Zuk 1986
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001a
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001b
;
Salcedo 2001
;
Osipov and Volkov 2017
;
Osipov et al. 2004
;
Vassilevich 2003
;
Dunne 2008
. Such method has been widely studied and developed in the context of two and three flavor NJL-type models, including different types of quark interactions at the Lagrangian level
Osipov and Hiller 2001b
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001c
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001a
;
Osipov et al. 2004
;
Moreira et al. 2015
. When applying this expansion to such type of models, one considers a Wick rotation of the operator inside the fermionic determinant in order to make it possible to obtain its Schwinger proper-time representation. In general, one can write
Osipov et al. 2004
:

\displaystyle\ln\absolutevalue{ \det\qty[D] }=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\differential{t}}{t}\rho\quantity[t, \Lambda^2]\Tr\quantity[ \e^{- t D_\mathrm{E}^\dagger D_\mathrm{E} } ].

(44)

Here,
\rho\quantity[t, \Lambda^2]
is a function, often called regulator or kernel, which incorporates the necessary regularization
Osipov and Hiller 2001a
,
\Tr\quantity[\ldots]
stands for the total trace and the operator
D_{\mathrm{E}}
, is the Wick rotated Dirac operator in the presence of background fields
\sigma=\sigma_{a}\lambda_{a}
and
\pi=\pi_{a}\lambda_{a}
. Additionally,
D_{\mathrm{E}}^{\dagger}D_{\mathrm{E}}=M^{2}-\partial^{2}+Y
, with
M=M_{a}\lambda_{a}
the effective quark mass matrix and
Y
contains only contributions coming from the background fields
Osipov et al. 2004
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001b
. In the case in which the quark mass matrix is not diagonal and degenerate, it does not commute with the rest of the operator and a resummation technique was developed in order to solve this difficulty
Osipov and Hiller 2001b
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001c
;
Osipov and Hiller 2001a
.

Applying the heat kernel expansion to Eq. (
17
), would lead to a different model, with different meson propagators when compared to our approach, which are equivalent to the ones obtained via the Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation. Thus, it could be insightful to evaluate the NJL quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections from meson propagators derived from a heat kernel expansion. The results of such study could then be directly compared to the ones obtained in this work. This could be used to assess the impact of considering different approximations to extract meson propagators from a purely fermionic theory as is the case of the NJL model.

II.3
The 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization

As previously established, in a 4-dimensional spacetime, the one and the two fermion lines integrals (
A
and
B_{0}
) are divergent quantities. Other integrals with higher fermion line count, like the three fermion line integral (
C_{0}
) are, on the other hand, convergent. When faced with divergent integrals, some regularization procedure must be applied. Then, the regularization procedure becomes part of the model in question
Ripka
and should be carried out in a symmetry preserving manner. The primary objective is to maintain the expected physical properties of the model unchanged after applying the regularization.

To attain such goal, a plethora of techniques was developed over the years: the 4-momentum regularization, the 3-momentum regularization, dimensional regularization, Pauli-Villars regularization, implicit regularizations and the proper time regularization
Meissner et al. 1990
;
Kahana and Lavelle 1993
;
Nikolov et al. 1996
;
Ripka
;
Inagaki et al. 2012
;
Inagaki et al. 2013
;
Kohyama et al. 2015
;
Torres Bobadilla et al. 2021
. The choice of regularization is of extreme importance since it introduces a scale, which informs about the range of applicability of a particular model
Ripka
. Such choice must also take into account the physical scenario under study. For instance, in quantum field theory at finite temperature and density, a compatible regularization scheme must be chosen, e.g., if a full sum over Matsubara frequencies is performed, one is implicitly considering the full range of the integration over the temporal component of the momentum.

The fact that the
A
and
B_{0}
are divergent, poses the following question: in a model calculation, does one simply regularize the diverging integrals, or is the regularization scheme applied to all integrals? For instance, in the NJL model, thermodynamic quantities like pressure and energy density contain both the well known divergent zero energy mode term (which corresponds to the vacuum contribution), as well as, convergent contributions (which are temperature and chemical potential dependent and can be regarded as in-medium contributions). The entropy density, in the same model, is defined by convergent contributions. In cases like this, one must choose where to apply the regularization (see for instance
Xue et al. 2022
). Indeed, in some works which only deal with the one fermion line integral, only the vacuum contribution of the
A
integral is regularized
Costa et al. 2010
. One can also find studies where the convergent contribution of the
A
integral is not regularized, but the convergent contribution of the
B_{0}
integral is, effectively mixing regularization strategies
Hansen et al. 2020
;
Costa et al. 2020
;
Soloveva et al. 2021
;
Xue et al. 2022
. In this work, as it was performed in Ref.
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
, we will use the Matsubara frequency summation to introduce temperature and apply the 3-momentum regularization to the remaining integrals. Furthermore, we choose to consider the regularization as part of the model, affecting all contributions, divergent and convergent alike.

At this point we introduce and discuss the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization which will be applied to the quark-antiquark polarization function
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
. In the 3-momentum regularization, the quark propagator is defined up to the maximum allowed momentum, the cutoff,
\Lambda
. In such scheme, a quark can have any 3-momentum inside a sphere of radius
\Lambda
. In the definition of the scalar and pseudoscalar polarization functions written in Eqs. (
28
) and (
29
), there are two quark propagators which must be integrated over a loop, one with momenta
p
and another with
p-k
, which carries an insertion from an external momentum
k
. In order to truly restrict the 3-momentum of the quarks, both momenta must be smaller than the cutoff,
\Lambda
. This means that the absolute value of the quark loop 3-momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}
, present in the integral of the polarization loop functions, must obey:
0\leq\absolutevalue{ \vec{p} }\leq\Lambda\wedge 0\leq\absolutevalue{ \vec{p} - \vec{k} }\leq\Lambda
. Such restriction can be visualized as the intersection between two spheres. Let
\mathbb{S}^{\Lambda}_{0}
and
\mathbb{S}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
be spheres of radius
\Lambda
, where the first is centered at the origin (
0
) and the second at a point distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
from the origin. We denote the intersection of these spheres
3
3

3

The volume of the intersection between two spheres of radius
\Lambda
, distanced k between each other, can be calculated to yield,
V_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}=\frac{1}{12}\pi\quantity(2\Lambda-k)^{2}\quantity(4\Lambda+k)
. In the
k\to 0
limit one trivially recovers the volume of a sphere of radius
\Lambda
,
V_{\mathbb{I}_{0}^{\Lambda}}=\frac{4}{3}\pi\Lambda^{3}
.
by
\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}=\mathbb{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
. Hence, the integrals present in the quark-antiquark polarization functions will be regularized by the intersection of these two spheres of radii
\Lambda
, distanced by
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
. An interesting property of the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization is that it becomes the usual 3-momentum regularization for zero external momentum: in such case both spheres completely intersect each other. This means that the one fermion line integral present in the quark-antiquark polarization function
\mathcal{A}[M_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]
, regularized with the sphere intersection scheme, degenerates to the usual one fermion line integral
A[M_{i}]
in the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\to 0
limit, i.e.,
A[M_{i}]=\mathcal{A}[M_{i},0]
. In Fig.
3
, we show the region of integration which regularizes the quark-antiquark polarization function,
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
. In the left panel of this figure, we show a particular 3-dimensional view of this integration region which corresponds to a “convex lens shaped” volume, defined as
\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
. In the right panel of the same figure, we show the two-dimensional cross section of the region in the
p_{y}-p_{z}
plane.

Figure 3
:
Region of integration in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme, used to regularize the quark-antiquark polarization function,
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
. In the left panel: a particular 3-dimensional view of the intersection of two spheres. The red sphere is centered at the origin (defined by the equation
\bm{p}^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}
) and the green sphere is distanced
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }
from it, in the
p_{z}
direction (defined by the equation
\quantity( \vec{p} - \vec{k} )^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}
). The blue region corresponds to the intersection between these two spheres and is designated by
\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
. In the right panel, we show the
p_{y}-p_{z}
cross section of the same integration region.

Another interesting property of this regularization scheme is its behavior for
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\to\infty
. As a matter of fact, for
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\geq 2\Lambda
, the spheres no longer intersect, the integration region disappears, the integrals
\mathcal{A}
and
B_{0}
are automatically zero and the quark-antiquark polarization functions vanish. So, the asymptotic behavior of the meson propagators are given by:

\displaystyle D^{S|P}

\displaystyle=\quantity[ \frac{1}{2} \qty(S|P)^{-1} - \Pi^{S|P} ]^{-1}\underset{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\to\infty}{\Rightarrow}D_{ab}=2\quantity(S|P)_{ab}.

(45)

Thus, for large momentum, the meson propagators converge to twice the meson projectors (
2S_{ab}
and
2P_{ab}
), which are temperature and chemical potential dependent quantities.

This novel approach to the 3-momentum regularization, can also be extended to integrals with three or more quark propagators present in the integrand: in such cases the region of integration is the intersection between the appropriate number of spheres. For example, for the three fermion line integral,
C_{0}
, three quark propagators would be present, meaning that the integral would be calculated over the intersection between three spheres of radii
\Lambda
. One sphere located at the origin, other distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
from the origin and another at distance
\absolutevalue{\vec{q}}
from the origin, with an angle
\delta_{\bm{k}\bm{q}}
between the 3-vectors
\bm{k}
and
\bm{q}
. In Fig.
4
, we show the
p_{y}-p_{z}
cross section of the integration region for the three fermion line integral, within the sphere intersection regularization scheme. We leave the details of the explicit calculation of the
C_{0}
integral, within this new regularization, to future work.

Figure 4
:

Cross section in the
p_{y}-p_{z}
plane of the region of integration for the three fermion line integral,
C_{0}
, in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme.

III
The in-medium two fermion, one loop integral

In order to introduce finite temperature, one first considers a Wick rotation to Euclidean spacetime, and then applies the Matsubara formalism (see for instance Ref.
Kapusta 1989
). Through this process Eq. (
31
) yields:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, i\nu_m, \abs{\vec{k}}]=16\pi^{2}T\sum^{+\infty}_{n=-\infty}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_i)^{2}-E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]^{2}}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_j - i\nu_m)^{2}-E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]^{2}},

(46)

with
\omega_{n}=(2n+1)\pi T
denoting the Matsubara frequency for fermions and
\nu_{m}
the Matsubara frequency for the external particle. Here, there are two distinct chemical potentials,
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
, each corresponding to different fermion masses,
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
and
E\quantity[ M, \vec{p}]
is the usual dispersion relation:

\displaystyle E\quantity[ M, \vec{p}]=\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+M^{2}}.

(47)

Applying a Wick rotation to the external momentum, one can perform an analytical continuation as,
i\nu_{m}\to k_{0}
.

One very important symmetry of this function arises when considering a shift in the Matsubara frequency as:
\omega_{n}\to-\omega_{n}+\nu_{m}
, which is allowed in the case of this infinite sum. Applying this substitution and performing the aforementioned Wick rotation, one can write:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]=

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_j,M_i,T,-\mu_j,-\mu_i, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}].

(48)

Hence, the two fermion, one loop integral
B_{0}
, is invariant with respect to the interchange of the masses,
M_{i}\leftrightarrow M_{j}
, and chemical potentials, albeit with an extra minus sign,
\mu_{i}\rightarrow-\mu_{j}
and
\mu_{j}\rightarrow-\mu_{i}
. Such symmetry can also be understood as exchanging the fermion in the upper line with the antifermion in the lower line in the loop diagram shown in Fig.
2
, where time flows from left to right.

When dealing with the integration over the 3-momentum,
\bm{p}
, it will become necessary to introduce an infinitesimal shift in the complex plane,
\pm i\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
, in order to deform the contour around singularities. As discussed in Ref.
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
, one can regard the masses as having an infinitesimal complex contribution,
M^{2}\to M^{2}-i\epsilon
, or consider other recipes, such as a shift in the zero component of the external momentum,
k_{0}\to k_{0}\pm i\epsilon

Fujii and Ohtani 2004
. The way in which the
\pm i\epsilon
term is introduced in the calculation has an impact in a very important symmetry property of the
B_{0}
function. In practice, choosing different recipes amounts to different time orderings of the single particle Green function. However, for the purposes of calculating meson masses in the NJL model and (differential) cross sections, the chosen
\pm i\epsilon
recipe will not change the results. The reason for this “recipe invariance” will be discussed later.

Considering the usual shift in the masses,
M^{2}\to M^{2}-i\epsilon
(see Ref.
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
), the
B_{0}
function takes the form:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]=16\pi^{2}T\sum^{+\infty}_{n=-\infty}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_i)^{2}-E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]^{2}+i\epsilon}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_j - k_0)^{2}-E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]^{2}+i\epsilon}.

(49)

Applying the simple change of variable,
\omega_{n}\rightarrow-\omega_{n}
, in the Matsubara sum, one can establish the following connection:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,-\mu_i,-\mu_j, -k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}].

(50)

Hence, for this choice of shift, the
B_{0}
function is symmetric with respect to the zero component of the external momentum,
k_{0}
. As discussed above, another way to introduce the shift is to consider it in the external momentum,
k_{0}\to k_{0}\pm i\epsilon
. In such case one can write:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]=16\pi^{2}T\sum^{+\infty}_{n=-\infty}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_i)^{2}-E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]^{2}}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{1}{\quantity( i\omega_n + \mu_j - k_0 - i\epsilon)^{2}-E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]^{2}}.

(51)

In this case, a different symmetry relation is obtained, involving the complex conjugate of the
B_{0}
function:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,-\mu_i,-\mu_j, -k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]^{\ast}.

(52)

We highlight that, differently from the previous symmetry, the masses and chemical potentials do not switch positions, one simply changes,
\mu_{i}\to-\mu_{i}
,
\mu_{j}\to-\mu_{j}
and
k_{0}\to-k_{0}
. In this work we will use the previously introduced mass shift however, to simplify the notation, we will only write the contribution
-i\epsilon
explicitly, when it is necessary. In later sections we will discuss why this symmetry is essential in order to maintain the symmetry between meson and anti-meson pairs and, consequently, provide a proper framework to evaluate the (differential) cross sections of different quark-quark and quark-antiquark processes.

The infinite sum over the Matsubara frequencies can be performed analytically, for example, by converting it into a contour integration on the complex plane. In such approach, the Matsubara sum is written as a sum of residues which, in turn, can be written as a sum of contour integrations where each contour surrounds a simple pole located at a particular Matsubara frequency. The contours can be deformed and the Cauchy residue theorem applied in order to obtain a result for the original Matsubara sum
Nieto 1995
. One gets:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]=16\pi^{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}\Bigg\{

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4E_{1}}\frac{1-2f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E_1-\mu_i,T]}{\quantity( E_1 - \lambda)^{2}-E_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{4E_{1}}\frac{1-2f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[E_1+\mu_i,T]}{\quantity( E_1 + \lambda)^{2}-E_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{4E_{2}}\frac{1-2f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[E_2-\mu_j,T]}{\quantity( E_2 + \lambda)^{2}-E_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{4E_{2}}\frac{1-2f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[E_2+\mu_j,T]}{\quantity( E_2 - \lambda)^{2}-E_{1}^{2}}\Bigg\},

(53)

where,
f_{\mathrm{F}}
is the Fermi-Dirac distribution function, given by:

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E , T ]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{E/T}+1},

(54)

and, for simplicity, we have defined:

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}],

(55)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}],

(56)

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=k_{0}+\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}.

(57)

Figure 5
:
Pair creation/annihilation processes
Fujii and Ohtani 2004
.

Figure 6
:
Scattering processes: absorption and emission
Fujii and Ohtani 2004
;
Mustafa 2022
.

As introduced in
Fujii and Ohtani 2004
;
Yamazaki and Matsui 2013
it is quite useful to write this function using the so-called pair creation/annihilation,
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
, and scattering contributions,
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
. In Figs.
5
and
6
, we show schematic diagrams representing the pair creation/annihilation and scattering contributions, respectively. For a deeper explanation about all these different processes see Ref.
Mustafa 2022
. Using this separation, we can write:

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j, k_0, \abs{\vec{k}}]=B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]+B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}],

(58)

where, each component is given explicitly by:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=16\pi^{2}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{4E_{1}E_{2}}\frac{1-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E_1 + \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E_2 - \eta\mu_j, T ]}{E_{2}+E_{1}+\eta\lambda-i\epsilon},

(59)

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity( 2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{4E_{1}E_{2}}\frac{f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E_1 - \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E_2 - \eta\mu_j, T ]}{E_{2}-E_{1}+\eta\lambda-\sgn\quantity[E_2 - E_1]i\epsilon}.

(60)

Here, we introduced the mass shift,
M^{2}\to M^{2}-i\epsilon

Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
, which will be necessary in order to use the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem
4
4

4

Introducing the mass shift and expanding the difference
E_{2}[\epsilon]-E_{1}[\epsilon]
around zero yields:

E_{2}[\epsilon]-E_{1}[\epsilon]=\quantity(E_2-E_1)-i\epsilon\frac{\quantity(E_2-E_1)}{2E_{1}E_{2}}+O\quantity[\epsilon^2].
Since
E_{1},E_{2}>0
, the sign of the first order term will depend on the difference of these energies and the denominator is absorbed by the infinitesimal
\epsilon
.

. The function
\sgn[x]
returns the sign of the quantity
x
, i.e.,
\sgn[x]=+1
if
x>0
and
\sgn[x]=-1
if
x<0
.

In the vacuum, the scattering contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
, vanishes and only
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
will contribute: in the vacuum there is no medium for scattering to occur, only the creation and annihilation processes contribute. This vacuum contribution is the only one which diverges. For cases with non-zero temperature and/or density the scattering term will contribute. In the later case, for
T=0
and finite chemical potential, one can introduce a Fermi momentum and easily perform two of the three integrations.

III.1
Practical implementation

III.1.1
The
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
case

Lets discuss the implementation of the
B_{0}
integral for the case where
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
. Since the integrand does not depend on the azimuthal angle, the integration over such variables is trivial, yielding a factor of
2\pi
. The remaining 2-dimensional integration is more complicated. One way to simplify the calculations and perform such integration, is to consider a change of variables. Here, we extend the approach made for the degenerate mass case (
M_{i}=M_{j}
) that was introduced in
Yamazaki and Matsui 2013
and also applied in
Câmara Pereira and Costa 2020
, by taking into account different masses. Consider the following change of variables:

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( E_2 + E_1 ),

(61)

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=E_{2}-E_{1}.

(62)

From these definitions one has the following relations:
E_{1}=E-\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}
and
E_{2}=E+\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}
. Looking at Eq. (
62
), the quantity
\varepsilon
can be both positive or negative while, checking Eq. (
61
),
E
is always a positive quantity. We also point out that the quantities
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are always positive since they are energies.

The integration measure will be changed as
\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}=\absolutevalue{ \det[ J ] }\differential{ \abs{\vec{p}} }\differential{ \theta}
, where
J=J\quantity[ \abs{\vec{p}}, \theta]=\frac{\partial(E,\varepsilon)}{\partial(\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta)}
, is the Jacobian matrix. Performing the derivatives, one can write:

\displaystyle\det[J]=\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }\frac{\bm{p}^{2}\sin[\theta]}{E_{1}E_{2}}.

(63)

From the above one can realize that this change of variables is only valid for the
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }>0
. We treat the case
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }=0
as a special case since it is simpler and a different change of variables must be used.

Considering the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization,
\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]\to\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
and performing the proposed change of variables, one can write the integrals as:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}\frac{1-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E - \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} + \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E + \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_j, T ]}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}-i\epsilon},

(64)

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}\frac{f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E - \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E + \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_j, T ]}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda-\sgn\quantity[\varepsilon]i\epsilon}.

(65)

The integration over the azimuthal angle yields
2\pi
