and the integration over the remaining variables corresponds to evaluating the integral over the 2-dimensional cross section of the intersection between two spheres (see Fig.
3
). We have written the 2-dimensional integration without referring the specific order of integration. In fact it is extremely useful to consider both order of integrations. For the pair creation/annihilation contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
, we will use the order,
\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{\varepsilon}
, while for the scattering contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
, the order
\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}
will be used. In principle, if the integrand is a well behaved function, switching the order of integration yields the same results
Nahin 2014
. The reason for using the two possible integration orders is twofold. First, it allows to perform one of the integrations analytically, resulting in only one numerical integration in order to evaluate the
B_{0}
function. Second, it allows for a straightforward isolation of the simple poles present at
E=\nicefrac{{-\eta\lambda}}{{2}}
and
\varepsilon=-\eta\lambda
, allowing the use of the appropriate numerical methods to evaluate such type of integrals. For convenience, we define the functions
g_{\mathrm{p}}
and
g_{\mathrm{s}}
as:

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=1-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E - \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} + \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E + \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_j, T ],

(66)

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E - \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_i, T ]-f_{\mathrm{F}}\quantity[ E + \nicefrac{\varepsilon}{2} - \eta\mu_j, T ].

(67)

For the pair creation/annihilation contribution, we write:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\frac{1}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}-i\epsilon}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{\varepsilon}g_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\frac{G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}-i\epsilon}.

(68)

While, for the scattering contribution, one gets:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda-\sgn\quantity[\varepsilon]i\epsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}g_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\frac{G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda-\sgn\quantity[\varepsilon]i\epsilon}.

(69)

Both integration regions and all the functions that define them,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, are explicitly derived in Appendix
C
. We point out that the integration endpoints will be functions of several variables, such as
M_{i}
,
M_{j}
,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and
\Lambda
. However, here, such dependencies are omitted for simplicity. The functions
G_{\mathrm{p}}
and
G_{\mathrm{s}}
have been defined as the innermost integration for each contribution, separately, i.e.:

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{\varepsilon}g_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E,\varepsilon],

(70)

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}g_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,E ,\varepsilon].

(71)

The reason for this separations is that these integrals can be computed analytically, simplifying the calculation of the
B_{0}
function. For the pair creation/annihilation contribution,
G_{\mathrm{p}}
, one gets
5
5

5

Numerically, for low temperatures, it can be useful to make use of the Puiseux series expansion of
\ln[1+x]
at
x\to\infty
:
\ln[1+x]=\ln[x]+1/x-1/(2x^{2})+1/(3x^{3})-1/(4x^{4})+O[1/x^{5}]
.
:

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle+2T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -E + \frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMin-\eta\mu_i )/T } ]

\displaystyle-2T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -E - \frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMin+\eta\mu_j )/T } ]

\displaystyle-2T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -E + \frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMax-\eta\mu_i )/T } ]

\displaystyle+2T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -E - \frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMax+\eta\mu_j )/T } ].

(72)

For the scattering contribution, one gets:

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]=

\displaystyle-T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -\depsilondEEMin- \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon+ \eta\mu_j )/T } ]

\displaystyle+T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -\depsilondEEMin+ \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon+ \eta\mu_i )/T } ]

\displaystyle-T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -\depsilondEEMax+ \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon+ \eta\mu_i )/T } ]

\displaystyle+T\ln\quantity[ 1 + \e^{ \qty( -\depsilondEEMax- \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon+ \eta\mu_j )/T } ].

(73)

In the case where a singularity resides within the integration bounds, the integration is made using the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem and the
B_{0}
function presents both real and imaginary components. The real part of the pair creation/annihilation contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
, and the real part of the scattering contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
, are given by:

\displaystyle\real[B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\mathscr{P}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\frac{G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}},

(74)

\displaystyle\real[B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\Big]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\mathscr{P}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\frac{G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda}.

(75)

The symbol
\mathscr{P}
stands for Cauchy principal value. The respective imaginary contributions are:

\displaystyle\imaginary[B_{0,\mathrm{p}}

\displaystyle\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\Big]=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}}, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }],&\text{if }\nicefrac{{-\eta\lambda}}{{2}}\in\quantity[\dEdepsilonEMin, \dEdepsilonEMax],\\
0,&\text{otherwise },\end{array}\right.

(76)

\displaystyle\imaginary[B_{0,\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\Big]=

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\sgn\quantity[ -\eta\lambda]G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},-\eta\lambda],&\text{if }-\eta\lambda\in\quantity[\depsilondEepsilonMin, \depsilondEepsilonMax],\\
0,&\text{otherwise }.\end{array}\right.

(77)

If one considers the external momentum shift,
k_{0}\to k_{0}\pm i\epsilon
, the real part of both contributions,
\real\quantity[B_{0,\pair}]
and
\real\quantity[B_{0,\scat}]
, remains unchanged, however, the overall sign in front of the imaginary contributions is altered. The imaginary contributions are changed as:

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}}, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }]

\displaystyle\to\mp\eta G_{\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}}, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }],

(78)

\displaystyle\sgn\quantity[ -\eta\lambda]G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},-\eta\lambda]

\displaystyle\to\mp\eta G_{\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}}, -\eta\lambda].

(79)

Finally, the
B_{0}
function can be written using the real and imaginary parts of the pair creation/annihilation and scattering contributions as (see Eq. (
58
)):

\displaystyle B_{0}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]=

\displaystyle\real[B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]+B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\bigg]

\displaystyle+i\imaginary[B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]+B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,\abs{\vec{k}}]\bigg].

(80)

III.1.2
The
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
case

The
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
limiting case cannot be calculated from the case
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
. As it was explained earlier (see Section
III.1.1
), the Jacobian determinant used when making the change of variables is zero for
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
(see Eq. (
63
)). Hence, the case
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
must be treated separately. Differently from the previous case, our calculation for the case
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
completely agrees with the one found in
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
. In this case there is no separation between the spheres and the region of integration is simply the one inside a sphere of radius
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\leq\Lambda
, the usual 3-momentum regularization. Nonetheless, for completeness, we will briefly show the explicit calculation.

It is easier to consider a particular change of variables for the integral
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
and a different one for the integral
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
. Furthermore, in this special case the integrand does not have angular dependencies, meaning that the integral over the azimuthal angle,
\varphi
, and polar angle,
\theta
, yield an overall factor of
4\pi
and an integration over the norm of the 3-momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}
.

Lets start by discussing the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
case of the pair creation/annihilation contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{p}}
by considering the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
limit of Eq. (
59
). Solving the equation,
E=\quantity(E_2 + E_1)/2=\quantity(E\qty[M_i,{\vec{p}}]+E\qty[M_j,{\vec{p}}])/2
, for the variable
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}
, one can consider a change of variables from
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\to E
. Using the positive solution, one gets:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}=\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( 2 E - M_i + M_j )\quantity( 2 E - M_i - M_j )\quantity( 2 E + M_i - M_j )\quantity( 2 E + M_i + M_j )}{E^{2}}}=P\quantity[M_i,M_j,E].

(81)

The integration measure,
\differential{ \abs{\vec{p}} }
, changes as,
\differential{ \abs{\vec{p}} }\frac{\bm{p}^{2}}{E_{1}E_{2}}=\differential{ E }\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,E]}{E}
and the integral is written as:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0, 0]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{E_{0}}^{E_{\Lambda}}\differential{ E }\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,E]}{E}\frac{1-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\overline{E_{1}}+\eta\mu_{i},T]-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\overline{E_{2}}-\eta\mu_{j},T]}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}-i\epsilon}.

(82)

Here,
E_{0}
and
E_{\Lambda}
are the endpoints obtained after the change of variables and are given by:

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(E\qty[M_i,0]+E\qty[M_j,0])=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( M_i + M_j ),

(83)

\displaystyle E_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(E\qty[M_i,\Lambda]+E\qty[M_j,\Lambda])=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
\sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } + \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_j^2 }
).

(84)

For simplicity, we introduced the functions
\overline{E_{1}}
and
\overline{E_{2}}
, which are the functions defined in Eqs. (
55
) and (
56
), written in terms of
E
instead of
\bm{p}
. They are explicitly given by:

\displaystyle\overline{E_{1}}

\displaystyle=\overline{E_{1}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,E]=2E-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity(4 E^2 - M_i^2 + M_j^2)^{2}}{E^{2}}},

(85)

\displaystyle\overline{E_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{E_{2}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,E]=2E-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( 4 E^2 + M_i^2 - M_j^2)^{2}}{E^{2}}}.

(86)

As for the case
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
, one can use the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem to write the real and imaginary parts, of the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
limit of the
B_{0}
function. We write:

\displaystyle\real[

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,0]\Big]=\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\mathscr{P}\int_{E_{0}}^{E_{\Lambda}}\differential{ E }\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,E]}{E}\frac{1-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\overline{E_{1}}+\eta\mu_{i},T]-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\overline{E_{2}}-\eta\mu_{j},T]}{E+\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{2}}},

(87)

\displaystyle\imaginary[

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{p}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,0]\Big]=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\pi\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }]}{\nicefrac{{-\eta\lambda}}{{2}}}\quantity( 1 - \nfermi[ \bar{E_1} + \eta\mu_i, T ] - \nfermi[ \bar{E_2} - \eta\mu_j, T ] ),&\text{if }\nicefrac{{-\eta\lambda}}{{2}}\in\quantity[E_0,E_\Lambda],\\
0,&\text{otherwise }.\end{array}\right.

Again, by considering the external momentum shift,
k_{0}\to k_{0}\pm i\epsilon
, instead of the mass shift, only the imaginary part is changed with the following substitution:
P\quantity[M_i,M_j, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }]\to-\eta P\quantity[M_i,M_j, \nicefrac{ -\eta\lambda}{ 2 }]
.

Finally, we deal with the scattering contribution,
B_{0,\mathrm{s}}
applying the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
limit to Eq. (
60
). For this case we consider a change of variables based on solving the equation,
\varepsilon=E_{2}-E_{1}=E\quantity[M_j,{\vec{p}}]-E\quantity[M_i,{\vec{p}}]
for the absolute value of the momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}
and get:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( \varepsilon- M_i + M_j )\quantity( \varepsilon- M_i - M_j )\quantity( \varepsilon+ M_i - M_j )\quantity( \varepsilon+ M_i + M_j )}{\varepsilon^{2}}}=P\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon].

(89)

In this case, the integration measure after the change of variables is,

\frac{\differential{ \abs{\vec{p}} }\bm{p}^{2}}{E_{1}E_{2}}=-\frac{\differential{ \varepsilon}P\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon]}{\varepsilon}
and the integral can then be written as:

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0, 0]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{\varepsilon_{\Lambda}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon]}{\varepsilon}\frac{f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{1}}-\eta\mu_{i},T]-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{2}}-\eta\mu_{j},T]}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda-\sgn\quantity[\varepsilon]i\epsilon}.

(90)

As before, the functions,
\hat{E_{1}}
and
\hat{E_{2}}
are the functions defined in Eqs. (
55
) and (
56
), written in terms of
\varepsilon
instead of
\bm{p}
and are given by:

\displaystyle\hat{E_{1}}

\displaystyle=\hat{E_{1}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon]=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( \varepsilon^2 - M_i^2 + M_j^2)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}}-\varepsilon,

(91)

\displaystyle\hat{E_{2}}

\displaystyle=\hat{E_{2}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon]=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( \varepsilon^2 + M_i^2 - M_j^2)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}}+\varepsilon.

(92)

The integration endpoints are defined as:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle=E\quantity[M_j,0]-E\quantity[M_i,0]=M_{j}-M_{i},

(93)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=E\quantity[M_j,\Lambda]-E\quantity[M_i,\Lambda]=\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}-\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}.

(94)

Following the previous steps, the real and imaginary parts can be written as:

\displaystyle\real[

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,0]\Big]=2\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\mathscr{P}\int_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{\varepsilon_{\Lambda}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,\varepsilon]}{\varepsilon}\frac{f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{1}}-\eta\mu_{i},T]-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{2}}-\eta\mu_{j},T]}{\varepsilon+\eta\lambda},

(95)

\displaystyle\imaginary[

\displaystyle B_{0,\mathrm{s}}\quantity[M_i,M_j,T,\mu_i,\mu_j,k_0,0]\Big]=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad 2\pi\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\sgn\quantity[ -\eta\lambda]\frac{P\quantity[M_i,M_j,-\eta\lambda]}{-\eta\lambda}(f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{1}}-\eta\mu_{i},T]-f_{\mathrm{F}}[\hat{E_{2}}-\eta\mu_{j},T]),&\text{if }-\eta\lambda\in\quantity[\varepsilon_0,\varepsilon_\Lambda],\\
0,&\text{otherwise }.\end{array}\right.

In the momentum shift case, the imaginary part is modified as:
\sgn\quantity[ -\eta\lambda]P\quantity[M_i,M_j,-\eta\lambda]\to-\eta P\quantity[M_i,M_j,-\eta\lambda]
. The complete function,
B_{0}
, for the case with
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
, can then be obtained as before, using Eq. (
80
) by using the real and imaginary parts calculated above.

In the Appendix
D
, we show and discuss some numerical results from evaluating the two fermion line integral,
B_{0}
for different values of temperature, chemical potential and external momentum by fixing a cutoff
\Lambda
and the effective masses,
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
.

IV
The in-medium one fermion, one loop integral

As discussed in earlier sections, different versions of the one fermion line integral arise when making calculations in the context of the NJL model. On the one hand, it appears in the calculation of the quark condensate (integral
A
defined in Eq. (
9
)) while, on the other hand, it contributes to the quark-antiquark polarization function (integral
\mathcal{A}
defined in Eq. (
32
)). In this work, we use the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization, implying that the integrals
A
and
\mathcal{A}
are different. The first,
A
, within this regularization, is integrated over the region of the intersection of one sphere centered in the origin and radius
\Lambda
, with itself, making it identical to the usual 3-momentum regularization. The second,
\mathcal{A}
, is a contribution to the quark-antiquark polarization function,
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
, and its integral is calculated inside the region of intersection between two spheres, distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
from each other, just like the
B_{0}
integral from the previous section.

Finite temperature can be introduced in the one fermion line integral,
A
(and
\mathcal{A}
), in the same manner as for the two fermion, one loop integral,
B_{0}
, see Section
III
. Applying these steps to Eq. (
98
) yields:

\displaystyle A[M_{i},T,\mu_{i}]

\displaystyle=16\pi^{2}T\sum^{+\infty}_{n=-\infty}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity(2 \pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{\quantity( i \omega_n + \mu_i )^{2}-E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]^{2}}.

(97)

Here,
\mu_{i}
is the chemical potential associated with the fermion of mass
M_{i}
. Performing the infinite sum over the allowed Matsubara frequencies yields the well known result:

\displaystyle A[M_{i},T,\mu_{i}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}[\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity(2 \pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]}\quantity(
1 -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i,\vec{p}] - \mu_i, T] -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i,\vec{p}] + \mu_i, T]
)

\displaystyle=-4\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\frac{\differential{ \abs{\vec{p}} }}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}}\quantity(
1 -
\nfermi\qty[ \sqrt{ \vec{p}^2 + M_i^2 } - \mu_i, T] -
\nfermi\qty[ \sqrt{ \vec{p}^2 + M_i^2 } + \mu_i, T]
).

(98)

Since the integrand only depends on
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}
, the angular integrations are trivial, leaving only one integration to be performed numerically. From the above results, the divergence of the vacuum contribution to the integral is evident and the necessity to regularize the integral is explicit. In the vacuum, one gets the well known result:

\displaystyle A[M_{i},0,0]

\displaystyle=-2\Lambda\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}-M_{i}^{2}\ln\quantity[
\frac{ \qty( \Lambda- \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } )^2}
{ M_i^2 }
].

(99)

Now, we consider the one fermion line loop integral which arises in the second order contribution,
\mathcal{A}
(see Eq. (
32
)). In the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme, we can write it as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity(2 \pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]}\quantity(
1 -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i,\vec{p}] - \mu_i, T] -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i,\vec{p}] +\mu_i, T]
).

(100)

As already mentioned, the region of integration
{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}
is formed by the intersection of two spheres, dislocated on the
p_{z}
axis (see Fig.
3
). In order to simplify the calculation, one can shift the region of integration in order to have the center of the intersection coinciding with the origin. This can be done by performing the following change of variables:
\bm{p}\to\bm{p}+\nicefrac{{\bm{k}}}{{2}}
. Using this transformation, the integral becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{\quantity(2 \pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E\quantity[M_i, \vec{p} + \frac{\vec{k}}{2}]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\quantity(
1 -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i, \vec{p} + \frac{\vec{k}}{2}] - \mu_i, T] -
\nfermi\qty[ E\qty[M_i, \vec{p} + \frac{\vec{k}}{2}] +\mu_i, T]
).

(101)

Here,
{{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}
is the new integration region constituted by the intersection of two spheres, one centered at
\quantity(p_x,p_y,p_z)=\quantity(0,0,\nicefrac{\abs{\vec{k}}}{2})
and other centered at
\quantity(p_x,p_y,p_z)=\quantity(0,0,\nicefrac{-\abs{\vec{k}}}{2})
, with the respective centers distanced by
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
. Interestingly, after performing the change of variables to center the region of integration and integrating over the azimutal angle, we are left with an extra dependence on the polar angle,
\theta
, making this integral slightly more complicated to calculate when compared to its analogue with only one sphere,
A
, given in Eq. (
98
). Using the steps shown in Appendix
C.3
(see Eqs. (
230
)-(
239
)), we can write this integral, in the vacuum limit, as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\Lambda-\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{1}^{-1}\frac{\differential{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}+\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\alpha+M_{i}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\int_{\Lambda-\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2}}^{\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{\frac{\Lambda^{2}-\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}-\bm{p}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}}}^{-\frac{\Lambda^{2}-\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}-\bm{p}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}}}\frac{\differential{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}+\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\alpha+M_{i}^{2}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}\Bigg\{\bm{k}^{2}\quantity(
\sqrt{ ( \Lambda- \abs{\vec{k}} )^2 + M_i^2 }
- 3 \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 }
)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\Lambda\quantity(
\sqrt{ ( \Lambda- \abs{\vec{k}} )^2 + M_i^2 } +
3 \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } )

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\quantity( \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 )\quantity(
\sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } -
\sqrt{ ( \Lambda- \abs{\vec{k}} )^2 + M_i^2 }
)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+3\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}M_{i}^{2}\ln\quantity[
\frac{1}{M_i^2}
\qty( \Lambda- \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } )
\qty( \Lambda- \abs{\vec{k}} - \sqrt{ ( \Lambda- \abs{\vec{k}} )^2 + M_i^2 }
)
]\Bigg\}.

(102)

In the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\to 0
case, the two spheres in the regularization completely intersect and one gets the expected result given in Eq. (
99
), i.e.,
\mathcal{A}[M_{i},0,0,0]=A[M_{i},0,0]
. Of course, this equivalence also holds at finite temperature and chemical potential as long as
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
.

At finite temperature and chemical potential it is not possible to write this quantity as an 1-dimensional integral using this approach. However, from the two fermion line integral
B_{0}
, we know that it is possible to further simplify this quantity, making an appropriate change of variables. For simplicity, we will use exactly the same change of variables performed in the case of the
B_{0}
integral, with
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
. Thus, considering the original form of the
\mathcal{A}
integral (see Eq. (
100
)), we multiply the integrand by one written as
1=E\quantity[M_j, \vec{p} - \vec{k}]/E\quantity[M_j, \vec{p} - \vec{k}]=E_{2}/E_{2}
(see Eq. (
56
)) and employ the change of variables used in Section
III.1.1
(see Eqs. (
61
)
-
(
63
)) in order to write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}\quantity( E + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} )\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
+
\nfermi\qty[ E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \eta\mu_i, T]
).

(103)

We highlight that this integral does not depend on the value of
M_{j}
introduced when one multiplies the integrand by
E_{2}/E_{2}
. Indeed, any value for
M_{j}
can be used in the calculation and this apparent extra parameter only arises in the calculation to allow us to use the formalism developed earlier in order to simplify the calculations. By analogy with the
B_{0}
integral, we separate this integral into two contributions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{p}}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]+\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{s}}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

(104)

where each contribution is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{p}}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{ E }E\,G_{E}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E],

(105)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{s}}[M_{i},T,\mu_{i},\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\varepsilon\,G_{\varepsilon}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon].

(106)

Here, we defined:

\displaystyle G_{E}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
+
\nfermi\qty[ E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \eta\mu_i, T]
),

(107)

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
+
\nfermi\qty[ E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \eta\mu_i, T]
).

(108)

As a result of making the appropriate change of variables, these integrals can be evaluated analytically to yield:

\displaystyle G_{E}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},E]=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\dEdepsilonepsilonMax-\dEdepsilonepsilonMin)

\displaystyle-2T\ln\quantity[ \e^{ \qty(\frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMax- E - \eta\mu_i)/T } + 1]

\displaystyle+2T\ln\quantity[ \e^{ \qty(\frac{1}{2}\dEdepsilonepsilonMin- E - \eta\mu_i)/T } + 1],

(109)

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}\quantity[M_i,T,\mu_i,\eta,\abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\depsilondEEMax-\depsilondEEMin)

\displaystyle+T\ln\quantity[ \e^{ \qty( \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon-\depsilondEEMax- \eta\mu_i)/T } + 1]

\displaystyle-T\ln\quantity[ \e^{ \qty( \frac{1}{2}\varepsilon-\depsilondEEMin- \eta\mu_i)/T } + 1].

(110)

In this way, the one fermion line integral, in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization, can be written as a sum of one dimensional integrals. Taking the vacuum limit, one can also perform the remaining integrations in order to obtain Eq. (
102
).

V
Cross sections for quark-quark and quark-antiquark processes in the NJL model

The goal of this section is to evaluate the cross sections of the NJL model and show the influence of the regularization scheme proposed in this work versus the 3-momentum regularization used in other works. We chose the cross sections to show the differences between regularization schemes because they constitute a physical quantity which is dependent on the absolute value of external momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
, present in the quark-antiquark polarization function
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
. As already discussed, the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme is dependent on the value of
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and, for
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
, one gets the usual 3-momentum regularization. Evaluating thermodynamic quantities (at mean field level, there are no external momenta), or meson masses (usually calculated at rest with
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
) would not yield any differences when compared to the usual 3-momentum regularization with a cutoff in all integrals.

V.1
The model

In order to calculate the cross sections for quark-quark and quark-antiquark scatterings, we will use the following
SU(3)
NJL Lagrangian:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\overline{\psi}\quantity(i\slashed{\partial}-m)\psi+\frac{G}{2}\Big(\quantity(\bar{\psi} \lambda_a \psi)^{2}+(\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}\psi)^{2}\Big)+8\kappa\Big(\det\quantity[ \bar{\psi} P_R \psi]+\det\quantity[ \bar{\psi} P_L \psi]\Big).

(111)

Here,
\psi^{T}=\quantity(\psi_u,\psi_d,\psi_s)
and
m=\diag\quantity{ m_u, m_d, m_s }
is the quark current mass matrix. This popular model includes, besides the free Dirac Lagrangian, a chirally symmetric set of interactions with a spontaneous chiral symmetry broken ground state in the vacuum. The choice of model is rooted in the goal to compare our results with other results found in the literature, where the regularization of the one fermion and and two fermion line integrals are performed in a different way than the one proposed here
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
. Hence, we only include in the Lagrangian the necessary four quark-quark scalar-pseudoscalar interaction and the ’t Hooft interaction
Rehberg and Klevansky 1996
;
Rehberg et al. 1996a
. However, the formalism developed earlier allows for a straightforward extension to include, at the Lagrangian level, multi-scalar and multi-pseudoscalar quark interactions, such as eight quark interactions or explicit chiral symmetry breaking interactions
Osipov et al. 2013b
;
Moreira et al. 2015
;
Morais et al. 2017
;
Câmara Pereira et al. 2020
. Indeed, in the future, we will consider an extended version of the NJL model which includes eight-quark interactions in order to study the influence of considering these higher quark interactions in the transport properties of the NJL model.

The central motivation behind the NJL model is the incorporation of a mechanism which induces spontaneous chiral symmetry breaking. In its original form this is achieved through the inclusion of a local four-fermion contact interaction term. One of the drawbacks of the model in this formulation is the fact that it exhibits a axial
U_{A}(1)
symmetry which is not observed in Nature (there are no degenerate mirror hadrons with opposite parity). The fact that no corresponding Nambu
-
Goldstone boson is observed led to the so called “
U_{A}(1)
problem” which can be solved by the anomalous breaking of this symmetry through instanton effects
’t Hooft 1976a
;
’t Hooft 1976b
. This mechanism can be incorporated in the model with the inclusion of the ’t Hooft determinant
2N_{f}-
fermion interaction term
Kunihiro and Hatsuda 1988
;
Bernard et al. 1988
;
Reinhardt and Alkofer 1988
. It should be noted that in the three flavor version of the model this has been shown to lead to vacuum stability problems which, in turn, can be solved with the inclusion of eight quark interactions
Osipov et al. 2006
;
Osipov et al. 2007
. In the present work, however, we will not consider these in order to enable a straightforward comparison of our results with the ones found in the literature.

Using the linear expansion of the Lagrangian, written in Eq. (
111
), as explained in previous sections, one can readily obtain the gap equations for this model:

\displaystyle M_{i}

\displaystyle=m_{i}-2G\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_i \psi_i }-2\kappa\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_j \psi_j }\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_k \psi_k }.

(112)

Here,
i\neq j\neq k\in\{u,d,s\}
. The value of the quark effective masses, at finite temperature and chemical potential, can be obtained by solving these equations self-consistently and the quark condensates,
\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_i \psi_i }
, can be calculated from Eq. (
8
). These gap equations can be obtained by calculating the thermodynamic potential of the model,
\Omega
, and requiring it to be thermodynamically consistent. This amounts to requiring the potential to be stationary with respect to the mean fields,
\expectationvalue*{s_a}
, i.e.,
\functionalderivative*{\Omega}{ \expval*{s_a} }=0
.

As pointed out earlier, and has it will become clear in following sections, to calculate the cross sections for different processes, one will also need to evaluate different meson propagators at finite temperature and density. The scalar and pseudoscalar meson propagators,
D_{S}
and
D_{P}
, were defined in Eqs. (
34
) and (
35
), respectively. The meson propagators are written in terms of the interaction-independent quark-antiquark polarization function,
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
, and of the interaction-dependent meson projectors
S_{ab}
and
P_{ab}
. As discussed in previous sections, the polarization function is independent of the type of interactions included at the Lagrangian level. As a matter of fact we derived it in Section
II.2
, without making any choice regarding the quark interaction Lagrangian. The meson projectors, on the other hand, are completely dependent on the type of interactions considered at the Lagrangian (as explained in Section
II.2
) and are defined as the coefficients in front of the quadratic products,
s_{a}s_{b}=(\overline{\psi}\lambda_{a}\psi)(\overline{\psi}\lambda_{b}\psi)
and
p_{a}p_{b}=(\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{a}\psi)(\overline{\psi}i\gamma^{5}\lambda_{b}\psi)
. As discussed, these coefficients arise when one makes the quadratic expansion of the product between quark bilinear operators (see Eq. (
13
)). The interaction-dependent meson projectors,
S_{ab}
and
P_{ab}
, for the Lagrangian defined in Eq. (
111
), are given by:

\displaystyle S_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
G \delta_{ab}
+
\frac{3\kappa}{2} A_{abc} \expval{s_c}
),

(113)

\displaystyle P_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
G \delta_{ab} -
\frac{ 3\kappa}{2} A_{abc} \expval{s_c}
).

(114)

Here,
A_{abc}
is given by:

\displaystyle A_{abc}=\frac{2}{3}d_{abc}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\quantity(
\delta_{a0} \delta_{b0} \delta_{c0} -
\delta_{a0} \delta_{bc}
-
\delta_{b0} \delta_{ca} -
\delta_{c0} \delta_{ab}
).

(115)

The quantities
d_{abc}
are the symmetric structure constants of the unitary group
U(3)
. As previously introduced, the expectation value
\expectationvalue*{s_a}=\expectationvalue*{\bar{\psi} \lambda_a \psi}
and we consider that there is no pseudoscalar condensation, i.e.,
\expectationvalue*{p_a}=\expectationvalue*{\bar{\psi} i \gamma^5 \lambda_a \psi}=0
. The non-zero scalar projectors,
S_{ab}
, are explicitly given by:

\displaystyle{S}_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
G
+
\frac{2}{3} \kappa\qty( \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } )
),

\displaystyle S_{11}

\displaystyle=S_{22}=S_{33}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G - \kappa\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } ),

\displaystyle S_{44}

\displaystyle=S_{55}=S_{66}=S_{77}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G - \kappa\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } ),

\displaystyle S_{88}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G - \frac{\kappa}{3} \qty( 2 \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + 2 \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } - \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } )
),

\displaystyle S_{03}

\displaystyle=S_{30}=-\frac{\kappa}{2\sqrt{6}}\quantity( \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } - \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } ),

\displaystyle S_{08}

\displaystyle=S_{80}=-\frac{\sqrt{2}\kappa}{12}\quantity( \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } - 2\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } ).

(116)

The non-zero pseudoscalar coefficients,
P_{ab}
, are:

\displaystyle P_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
G
- \frac{2}{3} \kappa\qty( \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } )
),

\displaystyle P_{11}

\displaystyle=P_{22}=P_{33}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G + \kappa\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } ),

\displaystyle P_{44}

\displaystyle=P_{55}=P_{66}=P_{77}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G + \kappa\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } ),

\displaystyle P_{88}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( G + \frac{\kappa}{3} \qty( 2 \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + 2 \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } - \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } )
),

\displaystyle P_{03}

\displaystyle=P_{30}=\frac{\kappa}{2\sqrt{6}}(\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u }-\expectationvalue*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d }),

\displaystyle P_{08}

\displaystyle=P_{80}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\kappa}{12}\quantity( \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_u \psi_u } + \expval*{ \bar{\psi}_d \psi_d } - 2\expval*{ \bar{\psi}_s \psi_s } ).

(117)

These are exactly the same as the one obtained in Ref.
Rehberg et al. 1996b
with a small caveat: in the NJL Lagrangian density defined in Eq. (
111
), the four quark scalar pseudoscalar interaction is proportional to
G/2
while, in Ref.
Rehberg et al. 1996b
, it is proportional to
G
.

At this point, one can explicitly write down the expressions for the meson propagators, defined in Eqs. (
34
) and (
35
). The indexes of the inverse meson propagators,
\quantity( D_S^{-1} )_{ab}
and
\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{ab}
, run from 0 to 8, the number of generators of the
U(3)
group. Thus, these objects can be considered as two matrices of dimension
9\times 9
. It is quite useful to write the scalar,
\Pi_{ab}^{S}
and pseudoscalar,
\Pi_{ab}^{P}
, polarization functions in the quark flavor basis, i.e., switch from
a,b=\quantity{0,\ldots,8}
to
\Pi_{ij}^{S|P}
, with
i,j=\quantity{u,d,s}
. Using Eq. (
29
), we can write the scalar and pseudoscalar polarization functions in the quark flavor basis. Explicitly, all non-zero elements are given by (for the sake of simplicity, we omit the scalar and pseudoscalar labels,
{S|P}
):

\displaystyle\Pi_{00}

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\quantity( \Pi_{uu} + \Pi_{dd} + \Pi_{ss} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{03}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{30}=\sqrt{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\quantity( \Pi_{uu} - \Pi_{dd} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{08}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{80}=\nicefrac{{\sqrt{2}}}{{3}}\quantity( \Pi_{uu} + \Pi_{dd} - 2 \Pi_{ss} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{11}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{22}=\Pi_{ud}+\Pi_{du},

\displaystyle\Pi_{12}

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{21}=i\quantity( \Pi_{ud} - \Pi_{du} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{33}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{uu}+\Pi_{dd},

\displaystyle\Pi_{38}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{83}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{3}}}\quantity( \Pi_{uu} - \Pi_{dd} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{44}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{55}=\Pi_{us}+\Pi_{su},

\displaystyle\Pi_{45}

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{54}=i\quantity( \Pi_{us} - \Pi_{su} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{66}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{77}=\Pi_{ds}+\Pi_{sd},

\displaystyle\Pi_{67}

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{76}=i\quantity( \Pi_{ds} - \Pi_{sd} ),

\displaystyle\Pi_{88}

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}\quantity( \Pi_{uu} + \Pi_{dd} + 4 \Pi_{ss} ).

(118)

All the other elements are zero. The polarization functions written in terms of the flavor indexes are not invariant under index exchange, i.e.,
\Pi_{ud}
and
\Pi_{du}
,
\Pi_{us}
and
\Pi_{su}
or
\Pi_{sd}
and
\Pi_{ds}
are not necessarily equal at finite temperature and chemical potential. Indeed, it is this difference which breaks the particle/antiparticle symmetry in the in-medium meson behavior
Costa and Pereira 2019
. For instance the charged kaons behave differently at finite chemical potential due to the mass difference between the quark and anti-quark particles which constitute the kaons: at finite chemical potential, the bath is more populated by particles than anti-particles.

Additionally, in this model, the inverse meson propagators are block matrices. Thus, we can write the second order term of the quadratic expansion of the effective action in Eq. (
33
) in different contributions. There will be a sector related to the indexes
a,b=1,2
,
a,b=4,5
,
a,b=6,7
and
a,b=0,3,8
. In other models, with different physical considerations (like considering pion/kaon condensation) or interaction terms at the Lagrangian level, this may not be possible. The sector with indexes
a,b=1,2
are related to the charged pion propagators,
D_{\pi^{+}|\pi^{-}}
. Consider the matrices
\lambda_{\pm}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}}\quantity( \lambda_1 \pm i \lambda_2 )
with
\lambda_{\pm}
creating a quark bilinear with the quantum numbers of the
\pi^{\mp}
, i.e.,
\pi^{\pm}=\overline{\psi}\lambda_{\mp}\psi
. Using these identities one can write the
\pi_{1|2}
fields in terms of
\pi_{\pm}
. Thus, one can write the
a,b=1,2
sector of the quadratic effective action of the model (defined in Eq. (
33
)) as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Q}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\bigg\{\pi^{+}[k]\quantity(
\frac{ \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{11} }{2} +
\frac{ i \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{12} }{2} -
\frac{ i \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{21} }{2} +
\frac{ \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{22} }{2}
)\pi^{-}[-k]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;+\pi^{-}[k]\quantity(
\frac{ \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{11} }{2} -
\frac{ i \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{12} }{2} +
\frac{ i \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{21} }{2} +
\frac{ \qty( D_P^{-1} )_{22} }{2}
)\pi^{+}[-k]+\ldots\bigg\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{Reg}}\frac{\differential[4]{k}}{\quantity(2\pi)^{4}}\bigg\{\pi^{+}[k]\quantity(
D_{ \pi^- }^{-1}
)\pi^{-}[-k]+\pi^{-}[k]\quantity(
D_{ \pi^+ }^{-1}
)\pi^{+}[-k]+\ldots\bigg\}.

(119)

Here, we have defined the inverse propagators of the charged pions as:

\displaystyle\quantity( D_{ \pi^+ | \pi^- } )^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{11}}{2}\mp\frac{i\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{12}}{2}\pm\frac{i\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{21}}{2}+\frac{\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{22}}{2}.

(120)

In this model, the inverse model propagator with indexes
a,b=1,2
is given, in matrix form, by:

\displaystyle\quantity( D_P^{-1} )_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
P_{11} & 0
\\
0 & P_{11}
\endlx@physics@matrix)^{-1}-\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix
\Pi_{ud}^P + \Pi_{du}^P & i \Pi_{ud}^P - i \Pi_{du}^P
\\
i \Pi_{ud}^P - i \Pi_{du}^P & \Pi_{ud}^P + \Pi_{du}^P
\endlx@physics@matrix).

(121)

We can then find that the charged pion propagators,
D_{\pi^{+}|\pi^{-}}
, are given by;

\displaystyle D_{\pi^{+}|\pi^{-}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2P_{11}}{1-4P_{11}\Pi_{ud|du}^{P}}.

(122)

Following these steps, one can find the remaining charged pseudoscalar meson propagators including,
K^{\pm}
,
K^{0}
and
\overline{K^{0}}
. They correspond to the
a,b=4,5
and
a,b=6,7
sectors of the pseudoscalar meson propagators matrix. They are given by:

\displaystyle D_{K^{+}|K^{-}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2P_{44}}{1-4P_{44}\Pi_{us|su}^{P}},

(123)

\displaystyle D_{K^{0}|\overline{K^{0}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2P_{66}}{1-4P_{66}\Pi_{ds|sd}^{P}}.

(124)

Likewise, the charged scalar particles can be obtained in the same manner. Their expressions are akin to the ones written above with the substitution of pseudoscalar quantities to scalar ones. The sector
a,b=0,3,8
, corresponds to the diagonal Gell-Mann matrices plus the scaled identity matrix, meaning they correspond to mesons constituted by linear combinations of
\overline{u}u
,
\overline{d}d
and
\overline{s}s
, i.e., the pseudoscalars
\pi^{0}
,
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
and the scalars
a^{0}_{0}
,
f_{0}
and
\sigma

Klevansky 1992
. Due to this mixture, one must use a diagonalization procedure in order to obtain the expressions for the propagators of these particles
Klevansky 1992
. For the purposes of calculating the cross sections of the NJL model, one does not have to calculate the propagators of these states. Indeed, as we will see, in the calculation of the differential cross sections, different mixtures of the elements of
D_{ab}
(with
a,b=0,3,8
) will be used, depending on the particular quark-quark or quark-antiquark scattering process considered
Rehberg et al. 1996b
.

V.2
Cross sections

In this section we lay out the analytical tools to calculate the differential cross section for several quark-quark and quark-antiquark processes within the context of the NJL model. Since these quantities depend on the meson propagator and furthermore, are functions of the external momentum of the propagator, they can be used to gauge the difference between using the regularization proposed in this work versus the usual regularization used in the literature. Hence, we will compare the cross section obtained in this work with the one provided in
Rehberg et al. 1996b
. The different cross sections can be obtained by integrating over the differential cross sections
Zhuang et al. 1995
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
;
Soloveva et al. 2021
.

(a)

t
-channel

(b)

u
-channel

(c)

s
-channel

Figure 7
:
The three possible Feynman diagrams involved in quark-quark and quark-antiquark scattering processes at leading order in an
1/N_{c}
expansion of the transition amplitude
Zhuang et al. 1995
(time is considered in the horizontal axis). The
t
- and
u
-channels arise in the scattering between quarks and the
t
- and
s
-channels arise in the quark-antiquark scattering processes.

As usual, in order to represent the different scattering processes, we will use the Mandelstam variables,
s
,
t
and
u
. These are defined by:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\quantity( p_1 + p_2 )^{2},

(125)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\quantity( p_1 - p_3 )^{2},

(126)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\quantity( p_1 - p_4 )^{2},

(127)

with the four-momenta
p_{i}

i\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
(
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
incoming particles and
i\in\left\{3,4\right\}
outgoing particles, see Fig.
7
). Momentum conservation implies the identity
s+t+u=\sum_{i=1}^{4}m_{i}^{2}
, allowing one to use the relativistic kinematics of the two-particle scattering to write all quantities of interest in terms of the
s
- and
t
-channels, eliminating the need to a direct reference to the
u
-channel quantities. By definition, the differential cross section is
\derivative*{\sigma_{ 12 \to 34} }{t}
. Thus integrating over the
t
-channel will yield the cross section of a given process as a function of the center of mass energy,
s
. In the center of mass frame it can be checked that the momentum of the exchanged particle, in the
t
-channel, is given by:

\displaystyle k_{0}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\quantity(
m_1^2 -
m_2^2 -
m_3^2 +
m_4^2
)}{2\sqrt{s}},

(128)

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( m_1^2 - m_2^2 - m_3^2 + m_4^2)^{2}}{4s}-t}.

(129)

In the
u
-channel, the momentum components are:

\displaystyle k_{0}^{(u)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\quantity(
m_1^2 -
m_2^2 +
m_3^2 -
m_4^2
)}{2\sqrt{s}},

(130)

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }^{(u)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\quantity( m_1^2 - m_2^2 + m_3^2 - m_4^2)^{2}}{4s}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}+s+t}.

(131)

Naturally, in the center of mass reference frame,
k_{0}^{(s)}=\sqrt{s}
and
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} }^{(s)}=0
. The scattering angle,
\Theta
, in the center of mass reference frame, can be written in terms of the incoming/outgoing masses and the
s
- and
t
-channels. It can be found to be given by:

\displaystyle\Theta=\arccos\quantity[
\frac{
\qty( m_1^2 - m_2^2 + s )
\qty( m_3^2 - m_4^2 + s )
- 2 s
\qty( m_1^2 + m_3^2 - t )
}
{
\sqrt{ \lambda(s,m_1^2,m_2^2) }
\sqrt{ \lambda(s,m_3^2,m_4^2) }
}
],

(132)

with
\lambda(a,b,c)=a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}-2ab-2ac-2bc
, the so-called triangle function. Thus, we can parametrize the differential cross sections in terms of the Mandelstam variables
s
and
t
.

At leading order in an
1/N_{c}
expansion of the transition amplitude matrix,
\mathcal{M}
, for the quark-quark scattering, only the
t
- and
u
-channels contribute while, for the quark-antiquark scattering, only the
t
- and
s
-channels are involved
Zhuang et al. 1995
;
Rehberg et al. 1996b
. The Feynman diagrams for the quark-quark and quark-antiquark scatterings can be observed in Fig.
7
. Taking into account all symmetries, at finite temperature and chemical potential there are a total of 6 different quark-quark processes, the corresponding 6 antiquark-antiquark processes and 15 different quark-antiquark processes. When dealing with exact isospin symmetry, at zero chemical potential, there would exist only 4 different quark-quark processes (
uu\to uu
,
ud\to ud
,
us\to us
,
ss\to ss
) and 7 different quark-antiquark processes (
u\overline{u}\to u\overline{u}
,
u\overline{u}\to d\overline{d}
,
u\overline{u}\to s\overline{s}
,
u\overline{d}\to u\overline{d}
,
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
,
s\overline{s}\to u\overline{u}
,
s\overline{s}\to s\overline{s}
).

12\to 34
process

\quantity(
\TCoeff{a}{f_3}{f_1}
D_{ab}^{P|S} [t]
\TCoeff{b}{f_4}{f_2}
)

ud\to ud
,
du\to du

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}-D_{33}^{P|S}+\frac{1}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}

us\to us
,
su\to su

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}D_{03}^{P|S}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}D_{38}^{P|S}-\frac{2}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}

ds\to ds
,
sd\to sd

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}D_{03}^{P|S}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}D_{38}^{P|S}-\frac{2}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}

uu\to uu

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}D_{03}^{P|S}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}+D_{33}^{P|S}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}D_{38}^{P|S}+\frac{1}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}

dd\to dd

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}D_{03}^{P|S}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}+D_{33}^{P|S}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}D_{38}^{P|S}+\frac{1}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}

\frac{2}{3}D_{00}^{P|S}-\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3}D_{08}^{P|S}+\frac{4}{3}D_{88}^{P|S}
