:

Cross sections (
\left[\sigma\right]=\mathrm{mb}
), as a function of the center of mass energy (
\left[\sqrt{s}\right]=\mathrm{GeV}
), for the
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
and
s\overline{u}\to s\overline{u}
processes, for
T=250\penalty\ \mathrm{MeV}
and increasing chemical potentials. The large dots mark the minimum center of mass energy requirements for a given processes to occur. Here, the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization is used.

We now turn our attention to the quark-antiquark cross sections. In Fig.
13
we have the processes with zero net strangeness,which include:
u\overline{u}\to u\overline{u}
,
u\overline{d}\to u\overline{d}
,
u\overline{u}\to d\overline{d}
,
u\overline{u}\to s\overline{s}
,
s\overline{s}\to u\overline{u}
and
s\overline{s}\to s\overline{s}
. The cross sections of quark-antiquark processes with strangeness are presented in Fig.
14
, and include:
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
and
s\overline{u}\to s\overline{u}
processes. As in the case of zero chemical potential, several of these quantities display a resonance structure due to the proximity of the values of temperature and chemical potential to the Mott temperature of the pions and kaons. Of course, when considering finite density, for every chemical potential one can find the respective Mott temperature. Thus, when considering in-medium systems, the melting point of the meson bound states is defined by a point in the space of possible thermodynamic variables, in this case, there will be a Mott temperature and a Mott chemical potential. The study of meson behavior in the medium, within the context of the model used here, is beyond the scope of this work and is left as future work. However, in the range of chemical potentials considered here, the Mott temperature of pions and kaons decreases with increasing temperature. Such effect can be clearly seen in some of the cross sections of Figs.
13
and
14
. The peak caused by the close proximity to the Mott temperatures of the pion and kaons gets smaller with increasing chemical potential. This can be observed in the cross sections of the following processes:
u\overline{u}\to u\overline{u}
,
u\overline{d}\to u\overline{d}
,
u\overline{u}\to d\overline{d}
,
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
and
s\overline{u}\to s\overline{u}
.

The kinematic divergence present in the exothermic cross section of the process
s\overline{s}\to u\overline{u}
is present for all considered chemical potentials however, it moves to smaller values of
\sqrt{s}
. The reduction of the strange quark mass, driven by the mechanism of restoration of chiral symmetry, is responsible for this behavior since the threshold energy for this process to occur gets smaller with increasing chemical potential.

The cross section for the
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
and
s\overline{u}\to s\overline{u}
are very similar. As mentioned, they also display a peak structure related to the proximity to meson melting points. However, the peaks are not located at the same points. These processes, in the
s
-channel, are mediated by the charged kaons, see Table
4
. While
u\overline{s}\to u\overline{s}
involves the positively charged
K^{+}
, the process
s\overline{u}\to s\overline{u}
exchanges a negatively charged
K^{-}
. Again, at finite density these meson are not degenerate, displaying different propagators, masses, widths and consequently, different melting points (labeled by a Mott chemical potential and temperature). Thus, in each process, the peak structure is located near its respective charged kaon melting point and their difference in essence, amounts to the charged kaons possessing slightly different melting points as chemical potential increases.

It is expected that the analyzing the effective meson-quark couplings at finite temperature and chemical potential
Costa et al. 2009
, could provide additional information regarding the fact that cross sections for quark-quark processes gets smaller with chemical potential while, in general, the antiquark-antiquark cross sections gets larger. However, such study goes beyond the scope of the present work.

VI
Conclusions

In this work we derived the one and two fermion line integrals from a general NJL model. We considered that the NJL Lagrangian contained the usual Dirac contribution for fermions fields and different scalar-pseudoscalar quark-quark interactions, contained in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}
. The formalism developed here is applicable to any number of scalar-pseudoscalar multi-quark interactions. For instance, one could consider the usual four quark scalar-pseudoscalar interaction, the ’t Hooft interaction, 8-quark interactions or explicit chiral symmetry breaking interactions. In order to derive the one and two fermion line integrals, we performed the linear and quadratic expansions of the product between quark bilinear operators in the mean field approximation. From these expansions one can extract thermodynamics quantities and meson propagators. The latter were then used to evaluate quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections at finite temperature and chemical potential. Furthermore, the meson propagators obtained in this work are equivalent to the ones used in other works where they are derived from the Bethe-Salpeter equation in the random phase approximation.

In order to perform these tasks, we introduced a new approach to control the diverging nature of the model, the so-called 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization. This new procedure is essential in order to maintain the symmetries of the model. For instance, it ensures that the cross sections for the processes
us\to us
and
su\to su
are the same for all values of temperature and chemical potential, which does not happen in the usual 3-momentum regularization.

As a practical implementation of the developed formalism, and to demonstrate the differences between the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization and the usual 3-momentum regularization, we considered the standard
SU(3)
NJL model, with four and six quark interactions
Rehberg et al. 1996a
, to evaluate quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections. We observed quantitative and qualitative differences when comparing quark-quark cross sections in both schemes. For quark-antiquark cross sections however, the results are similar. This is expected since, from the kinematic point of view, the differential cross sections for quark-antiquark processes do not have major dependencies on the absolute value of the external 3-momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
, meaning that both regularization schemes will give very similar results. This is a feature of the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization: it depends on the absolute value of the external 3-momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
. Since we are regularizing the integral by evaluating it in the region of integration corresponding to the intersection of two spheres with centers distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and radii
\Lambda
, when
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=0
, both spheres completely intersect and we end up with the usual 3-momentum regularization.

Thus, not only this new approach to the 3-momentum regularization scheme yields different quantitative and qualitative results for the quark-quark cross sections, it also provides a way to correctly account for the symmetries of the system. These points are very important when using the cross sections evaluated from this model to obtain the quark relaxation time and use it to predict the in-medium transport properties of the quark plasma. The impact of the regularization scheme proposed in this work on the evaluation of transport coefficients like shear/bulk viscosities and thermal/electric conductivities is currently being analyzed and being prepared for future publications.

As future work, we would like to use the Pauli-Villars regularization in order to regularize the model. We are specially interested on studying the differences to the cross sections, between using the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization, used in this work, and applying the Pauli-Villars regularization. As discussed, it is extremely important to use a regularization procedure which, like the one presented here, is able to preserve the symmetries between meson and antimesons.

Another extension of this work is to include the effect of magnetic fields in the calculation of the one and two fermion line integrals. Such extension can be very important to study systems under the presence of intense magnetic fields such as the case of heavy ion collision experiments or to study the matter inside magnetars. In such case, the Pauli-Villars regularization may be an extremely useful approach in order to study the effects of an external magnetic field. When considering a finite magnetic field, the 3-momentum integration is broken down in to an integration over the momenta aligned with the magnetic field, usually
p_{z}
, and the two dimensional integration over the transverse momenta,
p_{x}-p_{y}
, which is replaced by a sum over discrete values, the Landau levels. If considering a 3-momentum cutoff, the transverse component of the momentum should also be restricted, i.e., cutting off the sum. However, when dealing with an integration from 0 to infinity, as is the case in the Pauli-Villars regularization, this sum can be performed analytically, greatly simplifying the calculations.

We also wish to include in the model a coupling of the quark fields to an external static background of gluons. In such extended model, one is able to also study the deconfinement transition by studying the Polyakov loop order parameter at finite temperature and chemical potential. Such improved models are named Polyakov
-
Nambu
-
Jona-Lasinio (PNJL) models. In this work we used the pion Mott temperature as an indication for the system to be in the regime of deconfined matter. However, using the PNJL model would allow for a better description of the deconfinement transition and a better qualitative and quantitative indication of the presence of deconfined matter. Not only that, the PNJL is known to yield better qualitative results when compared to lattice QCD calculations, yielding a better qualitative prediction of the quark-quark and quark-antiquark cross sections. One can even use the so-called entangled-PNJL model where the coupling constants of the model are also dependent on the Polyakov loop order parameter.

Finally, the formalism developed here can also be extended to allow for the inclusion of pseudoscalar condensation at finite temperature and chemical potential, e.g., pion and kaon condensation
Barducci et al. 2005
;
Xia et al. 2013
. In asymmetric matter, such as the one created in heavy-ion collisions experiments or present inside neutron stars, the isospin chemical potential can be sufficiently large for meson condensation to occur
Albrecht and Erlich 2010
;
Florkowski and Abu-Samreh 1996
;
Khunjua et al. 2019
;
Andersen and Kyllingstad 2009
;
Xia et al. 2013
.

Appendix A
Linear expansion of the product between
N
commuting operators

An operator,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, can be written as its own expectation value, plus a small perturbation
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
around it
Pereira 2021
:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }+\quantity( \operator_i - \expval*{ \operator_i } )=\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}.

(156)

The perturbation is defined as:

\displaystyle\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}-\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }.

(157)

Using these expressions, we can write the product between two commuting operators,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
, as:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\quantity( \expval*{ \operator_1 } + \delta\operator_1 )\quantity( \expval*{ \operator_2 } + \delta\operator_2 )

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}.

(158)

The so-called linear product between these two operators,
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]
, is defined by neglecting the quadratic term
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
and then, re-writing the perturbation,
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, using its definition (see Eq. (
157
)). Thus, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }-\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }.

(159)

The process to define the linear expansion of the product between three commuting operators is exactly the same. Using Eq. (
156
), we can write the product between three operators as:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\quantity( \expval*{ \operator_1 } + \delta\operator_1 )\quantity( \expval*{ \operator_2 } + \delta\operator_2 )\quantity( \expval*{ \operator_3 } + \delta\operator_3 )

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}.

(160)

In this case, one neglects the terms proportional to both
(\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}})^{2}
and
(\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}})^{3}
. Then, using Eq. (
157
), one gets:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}.

(161)

This procedure can be performed to products with any number of operators. Thus, the linear product between
N
operators can be obtained, by the three following operations: firstly, writing each operator as in Eq. (
156
), secondly, neglecting terms proportional to
(\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}})^{n}
with
n\geq 2
, thirdly, writing the perturbations using the definition stated in Eq. (
157
).

Interestingly, one can obtain the linear product between
N
operators iteratively, by elevating the linear product between two operators,
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]
(explicitly given in Eq. (
159
)), to a definition. For completeness, one must also consider that the linear expansion of an operator or a constant (products of mean fields) is the operator or constant itself,
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}\right]=\hat{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{cte}\right]=\mathrm{cte}
. Using these definitions, one can build the linear expansion of any number of operators by iteratively using the following defining property:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right]=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N-1}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right].

(162)

Here, the particular set of
N-1
operators chosen to be inside the nested linear operation does not matter. Recursively, one can continue breaking down the innermost linear product, creating an iterative set of operations until the defining case of two operators is reached:
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]
. Explicitly, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right]=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\ldots\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N-1}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right].

(163)

For instance, following these definitions, the linear expansion of the product between three commuting operators is given by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\right].

(164)

Here, it is highlighted the fact that the particular combination of two operators chosen in the inner linear operation does not matter. By substituting the definition of
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]
in the above (see Eq. (
159
)), one recovers the linear product between three operators obtained in Eq. (
161
). For instance, for four operators, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right].

(165)

Here, we only wrote one particular choice for the nested linerization operations. There are several other possible permutations among the operators that give the same result.

Due to its recursive nature, applying the definition given in Eq. (
162
) to get the linear product between
N
operators can be quite cumbersome. Fortunately, the linear product between
N=n+1
operators, with
n\geq 1
, can also be obtained via the following formula:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1} \frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
- n
)\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }.

(166)

This formula can be proved by mathematical induction. First, one considers the base case and show that the proposition is valid for
n=1
. Substituting
n=1
, in Eq. (
166
), it yields the linear 2-operator product given in Eq. (
159
). This establishes the validity of the formula for the
n=1
case. The next step is to assume the validity of the formula written in Eq. (
166
) for a given
n=m
, and then show that it also holds for the case
n=m+1
. Thus, one has to show that the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m+2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{m+2} \frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
- \qty(m+1)
)\prod_{j=1}^{m+2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }.

(167)

The right-hand side of this equation can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS}

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
+
\frac{ \operator_{m+2} }{ \expval*{ \operator_{m+2} } }
-
\qty(m+1)
)\prod_{j=1}^{m+2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }.

(168)

Now, consider the left-hand side of Eq. (
167
). Writing the
m+2
contribution in the product explicitly and using the defining property of the linear product between
N
operators given in Eq. (
162
), we can write the left-hand side as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}\left[\quantity(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1} \operator_i )\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}\left[\mathrm{L}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+2}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}
\frac{ 1 }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
\Linear{ \operator_i \operator_{m+2} }
- m \operator_{m+2}
)\prod_{j=1}^{m+1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }.

(169)

Here, we assumed that Eq. (
166
) is valid for some
m
and used the fact that
\mathrm{L}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+2}\right]=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+2}
. The first term can be expanded using the base case, i.e., the result for the linear product of two operators given in Eq. (
159
). We write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS}

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}
\frac{ 1 }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
\qty(
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } } +
\frac{ \operator_{m+2} }{ \expval*{ \operator_{m+2} } }
- 1 )
\expval*{ \operator_i } \expval*{ \operator_{m+2} }
- m \operator_{m+2}
)\prod_{j=1}^{m+1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
+
\frac{ \operator_{m+2} }{ \expval*{ \operator_{m+2} } }
-
\qty(m+1)
)\prod_{j=1}^{m+2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }

\displaystyle=\mathrm{RHS}.

(170)

Since both sides of Eq. (
167
) are equal, the formula also holds for
n=m+1
, completing the inductive step. Hence, by mathematical induction, Eq. (
166
) is valid for
n\geq 1
.

Appendix B
Quadratic expansion of the product between
N
commuting operators

Similarly to the linear product between
N
commuting operators, the quadratic product between
N
commuting operators can be be obtained by writing each operator as in Eq. (
156
), neglecting terms proportional to cubic perturbations or higher,
(\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}})^{n}
for
n\geq 3
, and then re-writing the perturbations using Eq. (
157
).

Consider the product between three operators,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}
. It was explicitly written out in Eq. (
160
). Neglecting the term
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}
and re-writing the perturbations, yields its quadratic expansion:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_1 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_2 }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_3 }.

(171)

Following the same steps, the quadratic product between four operators can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]=

\displaystyle-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}-2\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}

\displaystyle+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}+\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle+3\expectationvalue*{\operator_1}\expectationvalue*{\operator_2}\expectationvalue*{\operator_3}\expectationvalue*{\operator_4}.

(172)

In order to write this expression, the terms proportional to both
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}
and
\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\delta\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}
were neglected, after each operator in the product
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}
was written using Eq. (
156
).

In a similar way to the linear expansion of the product between
N
operators described in the previous section, one can obtain the quadratic product between
N
operators iteratively, by taking the quadratic product between three operators,
\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]
, as a definition. Additionally, one must consider the following statements:
\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\right]=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
,
\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}\right]=\hat{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{cte}\right]=\mathrm{cte}
. Using these constraints, the quadratic expansion of the product of any number of operators can be obtained recursively using the following defining property:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right]=\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N-1}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right].

(173)

The particular set of
N-1
operators, chosen to be inside the nested quadratic operation, does not matter. Recursively, one can continue breaking down the innermost quadratic product, creating an iterative set of operations until the defining case is reached:
\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]
. Explicitly, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right]=\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{Q}\left[\ldots\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N-1}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\right].

(174)

As an example, the quadratic product between four operators, can be obtained by writing:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\right].

(175)

Substituting
\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\right]
by its definition, given in Eq. (
171
), one recovers the expression given in Eq. (
172
).

Instead of using the recursive approach laid out above, the quadratic product between
N=n+2
operators, with
n\geq 1
, can be conveniently obtained using the following formula:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n+2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}
\sum_{j=1}^{n+2}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
\frac{ \operator_j }{ \expval*{ \operator_j } }
\qty( 1 - \delta_{ij} )
- n
\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
+\frac{n}{2} \qty(n+1)
)\prod_{k=1}^{n+2}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }.

(176)

As for the linear product expansion, this formula can be proved by mathematical induction. First we consider the base case and show that the expression is valid for
n=1
, i.e., for the quadratic expansion of the product between three operators. Using this formula one can readily obtain the result presented in Eq. (
171
), validating the formula for the base case with
n=1
. Next, assuming that the formula holds for
n=m
, one must demonstrate that it is also valid for
n=m+1
. Hence, we have to show that the following relations holds (
n=m+1
case):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m+3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\quantity(
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{i=1}^{m+3}
\sum_{j=1}^{m+3}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
\frac{ \operator_j }{ \expval*{ \operator_j } }
\qty( 1 - \delta_{ij} )
- \qty(m+1)
\sum_{i=1}^{m+3}
\frac{ \operator_i }{ \expval*{ \operator_i } }
+\frac{\qty(m+1)}{2} \qty(m+2)
)\prod_{k=1}^{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }.

(177)

Let us focus on the right-hand side of Eq. (
177
). Separating the
m+3
contribution in the sums, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS}=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}

\displaystyle\qquad-\quantity(m+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}-\quantity(m+1)\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}+\frac{\quantity(m+1)}{2}\quantity(m+2)\bigg)\prod_{k=1}^{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }.

(178)

Consider the left-hand side of Eq. (
177
). One can separate the
i=m+3
contribution to the product and, using the defining property of Eq. (
173
), write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Q}\left[\quantity(\prod_{i=1}^{m+2} \operator_i )\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Q}\left[\mathrm{Q}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m+2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\right]\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\right]

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\right]}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-m\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}+\frac{m}{2}\quantity(m+1)\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\Bigg)\prod_{k=1}^{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }.

(179)

Here, we applied the base case (see Eq.(
176
)). Defining the first term inside the brackets by
t
, we can write it as:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\mathrm{Q}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\right]}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} ).

(180)

Using Eq. (
171
), we can write
t
as:

\displaystyle t=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{1}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{1}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }}\frac{1}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )\Bigg(

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }+\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }

\displaystyle\quad-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }+\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }\Bigg).

(181)

After some straightforward algebra, we can write
t
as:

\displaystyle t=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )

\displaystyle+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\quantity(m+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}

\displaystyle-\quantity(m+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\quantity(m+2)\quantity(m+1)\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}+\frac{1}{2}\quantity(m+2)\quantity(m+1).

(182)

Where we have used,
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m+2}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )=\quantity(m+2)\quantity(m+1)
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )=\quantity(m+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}
. Finally, the left-hand side of Eq. (
177
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS}=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_j }}\quantity( 1 - \delta_{ij} )+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}

\displaystyle\qquad-\quantity(m+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m+2}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_i }}-\quantity(m+1)\frac{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{m+3}}{\expectationvalue*{ \operator_{m+3} }}+\frac{\quantity(m+1)}{2}\quantity(m+2)\bigg)\prod_{k=1}^{m+3}\expectationvalue*{ \operator_k }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS}.

(183)

Hence, the left-hand side is equal to the right-hand side given in Eq. (
178
), the formula also holds for
n=m+1
, completing the inductive step. Hence, by mathematical induction, Eq. (
176
) is valid for
n\geq 1
.

Appendix C
Defining the region of integration for the
B_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}
functions after changing variables

In order to complete the change of variables performed in the two fermion, one loop integral (in the
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}>0
case) defined in Eqs. (
61
)
-
(
65
), one must find the limits of integration. The case of equal masses was performed in Ref.
Yamazaki and Matsui 2013
. By construction, the following equality holds,
E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]^{2}-E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]^{2}=\bm{k}^{2}-2\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos\quantity[\theta]-M_{i}^{2}+M_{j}^{2}=2E\varepsilon
, with
E\quantity[ M, \vec{p}]
defined in Eq. (
47
) while,
E
and
\varepsilon
, are the new integration variables, defined in Eqs. (
61
) and (
62
), respectively. Solving this equation with respect to
\cos\quantity[\theta]
and squaring both sides, yields:

\displaystyle\cos\quantity[\theta]^{2}=\frac{\quantity( \vec{k}^2 - M_i^2 + M_j^2 - 2 E \varepsilon)^{2}}{4\bm{p}^{2}\bm{k}^{2}}.

(184)

In order to have a right-hand side which depends only on the new integration variables and the external 3-momentum,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
, we can solve
E\quantity[ M_i, \vec{p}]=\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}=\quantity( E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} )
for
\bm{p}^{2}
(see Eqs. (
61
) and (
62
)) and substitute in the above equation.
Since, in the original integration bounds, the angle
\theta\in\quantity[0,\pi]
, it implies that
\cos^{2}[\theta]\leq 1
. This allows us to define the following inequality:

\displaystyle P\quantity[ \varepsilon, E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=\quantity( \vec{k}^2 - M_i^2 + M_j^2 - 2 E \varepsilon)^{2}-4\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( \qty( E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} )^2 - M_i^2 )\leq 0.

(185)

In the above, for simplicity, we have defined the function
P\quantity[ \varepsilon, E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]
. It is easier to obtain the integration region if we write the function
P\quantity[ \varepsilon, E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]
as a polynomial in
\varepsilon
, with coefficients which depend on the remaining variables,
E
,
M_{i}
,
M_{j}
and
\bm{k}^{2}
. The resulting inequality is quadratic in
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle P\quantity[ \varepsilon, E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=C_{0}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]+C_{1}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]\varepsilon+C_{2}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]\varepsilon^{2}\leq 0,

(186)

where,

\displaystyle C_{0}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=\bm{k}^{4}+2\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( M_i^2 + M_j^2 -2 E^2 )+\quantity( M_i^2 - M_j^2 )^{2},

(187)

\displaystyle C_{1}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=4E\quantity( M_i^2 - M_j^2 ),

(188)

\displaystyle C_{2}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=4E^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}.

(189)

The concavity of the inequality can be found by checking the sign of the coefficient in front of the term proportional to
\varepsilon^{2}
,
C_{2}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]
. One can show that this coefficient is always bigger than zero due to the so-called triangle inequality: in a normed vector space
V
, one can write the triangle inequality:
\absolutevalue{ \vec{x} + \vec{y} }\leq\absolutevalue{\vec{x}}+\absolutevalue{\vec{y}}
, for all
\bm{x}
,
\bm{y}
that belongs to
V
. Trivially, we can write
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=\absolutevalue{ \qty(\vec{k} - \vec{p}) + \vec{p} }
and, making use of the triangle inequality, it follows that:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} - \vec{p} }+\absolutevalue{\vec{p} }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bm{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\quantity( \abs{ \vec{k} - \vec{p} } )^{2}+\quantity( \abs{\vec{p} } )^{2}+2\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} - \vec{p} }\absolutevalue{ \vec{p} }.

(190)

One can always write
\absolutevalue{ \vec{p} }\leq\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}=E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]
and
\absolutevalue{ \vec{k} - \vec{p} }\leq\sqrt{\quantity(\vec{k-p})^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}=E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]
, since
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
are real and
M_{i},M_{j}>0
. The above inequality then becomes:

\displaystyle\bm{k}^{2}

\displaystyle<\quantity(
E[M_j,\vec{p}-\vec{k}]
+
E\qty[M_i,\vec{p}]
)^{2}=4E^{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow 0

\displaystyle<4E^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}.

(191)

Thus, the coefficient of the quadratic term,
C_{2}\quantity[ E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=4E^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}
, is always positive, meaning that it corresponds to a parabola with the concavity turned upwards. Hence, for every fixed value of
E
, the region of integration is an upwards parabola. Since we are interested in the region delimited by the values of
\varepsilon
and
E
for which
P\quantity[ \varepsilon, E, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]\leq 0
, this implies that the region of integration corresponds to the interior of the parabola (the region between the two zeros).

To finish determining the integration region, we need to apply the restriction coming from the 3-momentum cutoff in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization (see Section
II.3
). These restrictions can readily be found by demanding that,
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\leq\Lambda
and
\absolutevalue{\vec{p}-\vec{k}}\leq\Lambda
. For the first constraint, we get:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\leq\Lambda\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quantity( E - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} )^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}\Rightarrow-\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}\leq E-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}}.

(192)

For the second constraint, one gets:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\vec{p}-\vec{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\Rightarrow\quantity( E + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} )^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}\Rightarrow-\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}\leq E+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}.

(193)

At this point, one specific choice of integration order must be chosen in order to evaluate the integral. As previously discussed it is quite useful to utilize both integration orders since, in certain terms, one can analytically perform the innermost integration when using the order
\int\differential{ \varepsilon}\int\differential{ E }
while, for other terms, this analytical integration can only be performed for the other integration order,
\int\differential{ E }\int\differential{ \varepsilon}
.

It will also be quite useful to evaluate the integral
R_{ij}
, defined by:

\displaystyle R_{ij}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{E\quantity[M_i,\vec{p}]E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\bm{k}]}.

(194)

Where the integration is to be made considering the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization. Changing to the
E
and
\varepsilon
variables (see Eqs. (
61
) and (
62
) and (
63
)), one obtains:

\displaystyle R_{ij}\quantity[\Lambda,M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}.

(195)

Hence, one immediately finds that
2\pi R_{ij}
corresponds to the volume of the integration region delimited by the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization in the new variables
E
and
\varepsilon
. Evaluating this quantity will be quite useful in order to:
(1)
check that the change of variables is valid;
(2)
verify that both integration orders,
\int\differential{ \varepsilon}\int\differential{ E }
and
\int\differential{ E }\int\differential{ \varepsilon}
, yield the same results. In a later section we will also show how to evaluate this integral (
R_{ij}
), without making the change of variables, allowing for a multiple numerical cross check for the validity of the proposed integration procedure.

C.1
Integration order
\differential{ \varepsilon}\differential{ E }

In this section we will consider the
\differential{ \varepsilon}\differential{ E }
integration order, hence we will integrate first over the
E
variable. In other words, we write the integration of a function
f(E,\varepsilon)
, as:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ \varepsilon}\differential{ E }f(E,\varepsilon)=\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{\varepsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}f(E,\varepsilon)

(196)

and define the different functions,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, which encompass the integration region and are functions of the masses (
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
), the absolute value of the external 3-momentum (
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
), and the 3-momentum cutoff,
\Lambda
. In the special case in which
f(E,\varepsilon)=1
, one obviously gets the integral
R_{ij}
, see Eq. (
195
).

To achieve this, we start by solving Eq. (
185
) with respect to
E
. This yields two solutions (
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}
):

E_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=\begin{cases}-\frac{(M_{i}^{2}-M_{j}^{2})}{4\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon\quantity( M_i^2 + M_j^2 )}{2(M_{i}^{2}-M_{j}^{2})},&\text{if}\;\varepsilon=\pm\absolutevalue{\vec{k}},\\
\frac{\varepsilon(M_{i}^{2}-M_{j}^{2})\pm\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( \vec{k}^2 + ( M_i - M_j )^2 -\varepsilon^2)\quantity( \vec{k}^2 + ( M_i + M_j )^2 -\varepsilon^2)}}{2\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} - \varepsilon)\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} + \varepsilon)},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(197)

In the special case in which
M_{i}=M_{j}=M
one gets:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}\quantity[M, M, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=\pm\frac{\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( \vec{k}^2 -\varepsilon^2)\quantity( \vec{k}^2 + 4 M^2 -\varepsilon^2)}}{2\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} - \varepsilon)\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} + \varepsilon)}.

(198)

From the restrictions imposed by the cutoff in Eqs. (
192
) and (
193
), one can further constrain the integration region. One can define the following function from solving theses equation with respect to
E
:

\displaystyle E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{\pm}[\Lambda,M_{i},\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}},

(199)

\displaystyle E_{\Lambda_{j}}^{\pm}[\Lambda,M_{j},\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}.

(200)

By definition, the variable
E
is positive (see Eqs. (
55
), (
56
) and (
61
)). Hence, we are interested only on the functions,
E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{+}
and
E_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
. Furthermore, these functions intersect at a certain point,
\quantity(\varepsilon_{\Lambda_s},E_{\Lambda_s})
. Such point is the solution to the equation,
E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{+}=E_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
and it is given by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{s}}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}-\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}},

(201)

\displaystyle E_{\Lambda_{s}}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity( \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 } + \sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_j^2} ).

(202)

Also, at this point, the restriction coming from the cutoff demands one to switch from one branch, to another. This can be more easily understood after a graphical analysis. Thus, we can unify the constraint coming from the 3-momentum cutoff in the following equation:

E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon]=\begin{cases}E_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{j},\varepsilon],&\text{if}\;\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{s}}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j],\\
E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{i},\varepsilon],&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(203)

At this point we can define the lower bound of the
\differential{E}
integration,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
. It is simply given by:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=E_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon].

(204)

The upper bound of the
\differential{E}
integration,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, however, is not so simple. When dealing with equal fermion masses (
M_{i}=M_{j}=M
), the constraint coming from the cutoff defined previously, completely fixes the upper bound. However, when dealing with different masses, this is not true and a switch from
E_{\Lambda}
to the function
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
, can occur. This is also better understood after a visual analysis of the interplay between the functions. In order to define
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
we will need to find other quantities of interest where some of the previously mentioned quantities intersect. Firstly, consider a possible intersection between the function
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
and
E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{+}
, i.e., solutions of the equation,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon_{\gamma_1}]=E_{\Lambda_{i}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{i},\varepsilon_{\gamma_{1}}]
. This results in four different intersection points, with only one being meaningful to the integration region (the other ones are outside the region). Its
\quantity(\varepsilon_{\gamma_1},E_{\gamma_1})
values are given by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} - \Lambda)^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}-\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}},

(205)

\displaystyle E_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
\sqrt{ \qty( \abs{\vec{k}} - \Lambda)^2 + M_j^2 }
+
\sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_i^2 }
).

(206)

Likewise, the solution of interest to the equation
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon_{\gamma_2}]=E_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{j},\varepsilon_{\gamma_{2}}]
is:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}-\sqrt{\quantity( \abs{\vec{k}} - \Lambda)^{2}+M_{i}^{2}},

(207)

\displaystyle E_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(
\sqrt{ \Lambda^2 + M_j^2 }
+
\sqrt{ \qty( \abs{\vec{k}} - \Lambda)^2 + M_i^2 }
).

(208)

Secondly, the equations
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}=E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
are equal in four different points, with two of them being useful for our purposes,
\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}}
and
\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}}
. They can be calculated to yield:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}+(M_{i}-M_{j})^{2}}=-\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ].

(209)

To each of these
\varepsilon
points there are corresponding
E
values, given by:

\displaystyle E_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\quantity( M_i + M_j )}{\quantity( M_i - M_j )}\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}+(M_{i}-M_{j})^{2}}=-E_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ].

(210)

From the above, one can observe that,
E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{2}}\to\infty
for the degenerate mass case, meaning that the two equations,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
and
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
, are never equal. However, for different masses, there will exist two switching points, symmetric with respect to
\varepsilon
(see Eq. (
209
)). Thus, as previously aluded to, increasing the difference between the masses, causes
E_{\alpha_{1}}
and
E_{\alpha_{2}}
to decrease and the upper bound,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, ceases to be defined by
E_{\Lambda}
switching to
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
. In the case where
M_{i}>M_{j}
, one can write:

E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(M_{i}>M_{j})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=\begin{cases}E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]\;\text{and}\\
\phantom{\text{if}\;}E_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]<E_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],\end{array}\\
E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(211)

In the opposite mass case,
M_{j}>M_{i}
, the upper bound is:

E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(M_{j}>M_{i})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=\begin{cases}E_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]\;\text{and}\\
\phantom{\text{if}\;}E_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]<E_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],\end{array}\\
E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(212)

As explained before, for the degenerate mass case, the upper bound is trivially given by the cutoff restriction only, i.e.,
E_{\Lambda}
. Gathering all this information, one can write the upper bound of the
\differential{E}
integration,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, as:

E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]=\begin{cases}E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(M_{i}>M_{j})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon],&\text{if}\;M_{i}>M_{j},\\
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(M_{j}>M_{i})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon],&\text{if}\;M_{i}<M_{j},\\
E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon],&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(213)

Finally, taking into account all the intersections of interest, one can write the lower bound of the
\differential{\varepsilon}
integration,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}
, as:

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;M_{i}>M_{j}\;\text{and}\\
\phantom{\text{if}\;}E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]]\geq E_{\alpha_{1}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],\end{array}\\
\varepsilon_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(214)

The upper bound, on the other hand, is given by:

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;M_{i}<M_{j}\;\text{and}\\
\phantom{\text{if}\;}E_{\Lambda}[\Lambda,M_{i},M_{j},\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]]\geq E_{\alpha_{2}}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],\end{array}\\
\varepsilon_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(215)

One can check that the size of the integration region gets smaller with increasing
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
. This is a consequence of the regularization used in this work. As explained earlier, increasing the value of
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
is equivalent to increasing the distance between the center of two 3-momentum spheres of radii
\Lambda
. For
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}=2\Lambda
the spheres no longer intersect and the integration region should be zero. Hence, to improve the numerical implementation of the calculation, one can additionally include a condition to only calculate the integral if
0<\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}<2\Lambda
.

In Fig.
15
we show the region of integration
\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}
, for fixed values of
\Lambda
,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
,
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
. We consider the three possible scenarios of
M_{i}=M_{j}
,
M_{i}>M_{j}
and
M_{i}<M_{j}
, in the left, middle and right panels, respectively. For simplicity, we fix the cutoff to
\Lambda=1\penalty\ \mathrm{GeV}
and consider different values of
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and masses. The goal is to provide different scenarios for easier reproduction of these results. We also show the values for the different areas resulting from integrating the region (see Eq. (
195
)). As discussed before, calculating these areas corresponds to evaluating the
R_{ij}
integral first defined in Eq (
194
).

(a)

M_{i}=M_{j}

(b)

M_{i}>M_{j}

(c)

M_{i}<M_{j}

Figure 15
:

Region of the integration
\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}
, for fixed values of
\Lambda
,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and different values of
M_{i}
,
M_{j}
, with
M_{i}=M_{j}
in the left panel,
M_{i}>M_{j}
in the middle and
M_{j}>M_{i}
in the right panel. The full black line is the upper limit of the integration over the variable
E
,
E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, \varepsilon]
(Eq. (
213
)) while, the dashed black line, is the inferior bound,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}},\varepsilon]
(Eq. (
204
)). The red dots represent the minimum and maximum values of the integration over
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]
, respectively (Eqs. (
214
) and (
215
)). In each plot we also give the values for the area of each integration region, which corresponds to evaluating the
R_{ij}
integral, defined in Eq. (
194
).

C.2
Integration order
\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}

In this section we will consider the
\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}
integration order, meaning that one first integrates over the
\varepsilon
variable. We write:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential{\phi}}{2\pi}\differential{ E }\differential{ \varepsilon}f(E,\varepsilon)=\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{\varepsilon}f(E,\varepsilon).

(216)

Again, if
f(E,\varepsilon)=1
, one recovers the integral
R_{ij}
, see Eq. (
195
).

For this case, the starting point is finding the solutions to Eq. (
185
) with respect to
\varepsilon
. This yields two solutions (
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}
):

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, E]=\begin{cases}-\frac{(M_{i}^{2}-M_{j}^{2})}{4E}-\frac{2E\quantity( M_i^2 + M_j^2 )}{(M_{i}^{2}-M_{j}^{2})},&\text{if}\;E=\pm\nicefrac{{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}}{{2}},\\
\frac{-2E\quantity( M_i^2 - M_j^2 )\pm\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( 4 E^2 - \vec{k}^2 - ( M_i - M_j )^2 )\quantity( 4 E^2 - \vec{k}^2 - ( M_i + M_j )^2 )}}{4E^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(217)

For equal masses,
M_{i}=M_{j}=M
, one gets:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{\pm}\quantity[M, M , \abs{\vec{k}}, E]=\pm\frac{\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}\quantity( 4 E^2 - \vec{k}^2 )\quantity( 4 E^2 - \vec{k}^2 - 4M^2 )}}{4E^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}}.

(218)

As before, the cutoff restrictions to the integration region can be obtained by solving Eqs. (
192
) and (
193
) with respect to
\varepsilon
. It yields:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{\pm}[\Lambda,M_{i},E]

\displaystyle=2E\pm 2\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{i}^{2}},

(219)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{\pm}[\Lambda,M_{j},E]

\displaystyle=-2E\pm 2\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+M_{j}^{2}}.

(220)

The upper and lower bounds of the
\differential{\varepsilon}
integration,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
can be defined from these equations. Starting with the upper bound, it is defined by the functions
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
and
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
which intersect at the point
\quantity(E_{\gamma_2},\varepsilon_{\gamma_2})
, previously defined in Eqs. (
207
) and (
208
). Hence, the upper bound of the
\differential{\varepsilon}
integration,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
, can be defined as:

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}},E]=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{j},E],&\text{if}\;E\geq E_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],\\
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, E],&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(221)

Similarly, the lower bound is defined by the functions
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
and
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}
. These functions intersect at the point
\quantity(\varepsilon_{\gamma_1},E_{\gamma_1})
, defined in Eqs. (
205
) and (
206
). Thus, we can write:

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}},E]=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}[\Lambda,M_{i},E],&\text{if}\;E\geq E_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],\\
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}\quantity[M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}, E],&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(222)

As with the integration order considered in the previous section,
\differential{ \varepsilon}\differential{ E }
, depending if one is considering equal or different fermion masses, the lower bound of the
\differential{E}
integration,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}
may be defined by the intersection of the functions
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
, by the intersection of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
with
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}
or by the intersection of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
with
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
. When the fermion masses are equal, the lower bound is given by the first intersection mentioned, i.e., when
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
. This occurs at the point
\quantity(E_\alpha,\varepsilon_\alpha)
defined by:

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}+\quantity( M_i + M_j )^{2}},

(223)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\quantity( M_i^2 - M_j^2 )\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}+(M_{i}+M_{j})^{2}}}{\quantity( M_i + M_j )^{2}}.

(224)

If
M_{i}>M_{j}
, the lower bound of the
\differential{E}
integration can also be located at the above intersection point,
E_{\alpha}
. However, for certain values of the parameters
\Lambda

M_{i}
,
M_{j}
and
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
, the lower bound of the
\differential{E}
integration can occur at the intersection between
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
and
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}
. This intersection also occurs at the point
\quantity(\varepsilon_{\gamma_1},E_{\gamma_1})
, defined in Eqs. (
205
) and (
206
). Hence, we can write:

E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(M_{i}>M_{j})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=\begin{cases}E_{\gamma_{1}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}[\Lambda,M_{i},\varepsilon_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]]>\varepsilon_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],\end{array}\\
E_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(225)

Likewise, for the opposite mass case, in which
M_{j}>M_{i}
, one can realize that the lower bound of the integration can occur at the point
E_{\alpha}
or, it may occur at the intersection between
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{-}
and
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
, defined by the point
\quantity(E_{\gamma_2},\varepsilon_{\gamma_2})
given in Eqs. (
207
) and (
208
). Thus, we write:

E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(M_{j}>M_{i})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=\begin{cases}E_{\gamma_{2}}\quantity[ \Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}\;\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}[\Lambda,M_{i},\varepsilon_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]]<\varepsilon_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],\end{array}\\
E_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(226)

Collecting these conditions, we can finally define:

E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=\begin{cases}E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(M_{i}>M_{j})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}],&\text{if}\;M_{i}>M_{j},\\
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(M_{j}>M_{i})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\text{if}\;M_{i}<M_{j},\\
E_{\alpha}\quantity[ M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ],&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(227)

The upper bound of the
\differential{E}
integration is much simpler to define. It is defined by the intersection between the cutoff restrictions, which takes place when
\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{i}}^{-}=\varepsilon_{\Lambda_{j}}^{+}
, in the formerly defined point
\quantity(E_{ \Lambda_s }, \varepsilon_{\Lambda_s } )
given in Eqs. (
201
) and (
202
). In such a way, the upper limit is then simply given by:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]=E_{\Lambda_{s}}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j].

(228)

In Fig.
16
we show the region of integration
\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}
, for fixed values of
\Lambda
,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
,
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
. Again, we consider the three possible scenarios of
M_{i}=M_{j}
,
M_{i}>M_{j}
and
M_{i}<M_{j}
, in the left, middle and right panels, respectively. It is now easy to check that one gets exactly the same areas (or, identically, values for the
R_{ij}
integral) using this integration order, for the same set of parameters, when comparing with the other possible integration order whose areas are shown in Fig.
15
.

(a)

M_{i}=M_{j}

(b)

M_{i}>M_{j}

(c)

M_{i}<M_{j}

:

Region of the integration
\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{E}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}
, for fixed values of
\Lambda
,
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
and different values of
M_{i}
,

, with

in the left panel,

in the middle and

in the right panel. The full black line is the upper limit of the integration over the variable
\varepsilon
,

(Eq. (
221
)) while, the dashed black line, is the inferior bound,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}},E]
(Eq. (
222
)). The red dots represent the minimum and maximum values of the integration over
E
,
E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{E}\differential{\varepsilon})}\quantity[\Lambda, M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}}]
and

, respectively ((Eq. (

)) and (Eq. (

))). In each plot we also give the values for the area of each integration region, which corresponds to evaluating the
R_{ij}
integral, defined in Eq. (
194
).

C.3
Calculating the integral
R_{ij}
without changing variables

In this section we show how to evaluate the integral
R_{ij}
, defined in Eq. (
194
), without changing to the
E
and
\varepsilon
variables. This is useful for numerical cross checks, as well as evaluating the vacuum limit of the one fermion line integral in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization,
\mathcal{A}
.

As discussed in Section
II.3
, using the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization amounts to performing an integral over the region defined by the intersection between two spheres,
\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
. One of the spheres is centered at the origin while the other is distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
from the first, in the
p_{z}
axis,
\mathbb{I}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}=\mathbb{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
. Such region can be observed in Fig.
3
. The major difficulty of evaluating the integral
R_{ij}
over this 3-dimensional region lays in the fact that it is not mirror-symmetric in the
p_{z}
axis. In order to make the region symmetrical we are allowed to consider, in Eq. (
194
), the following change of variables,
\bm{p}\to\bm{p}+\nicefrac{{\bm{k}}}{{2}}
. With this transformation, we are allowed to write the
R_{ij}
integral as:

\displaystyle R_{ij}\quantity[\Lambda,M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\frac{\differential[3]{\vec{p}}}{E\quantity[M_i, \vec{p} + \frac{\vec{k}}{2}]E[M_{j},\bm{p}-\frac{\bm{k}}{2}]}.

(229)

Where, the new region of integration, is defined by the intersection of the two spheres distanced
\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}
from each other, with one centered at
\quantity(p_x,p_y,p_z)=\quantity(0,0,\nicefrac{\abs{\vec{k}}}{2})
and the other centered at
\quantity(p_x,p_y,p_z)=\quantity(0,0,-\nicefrac{\abs{\vec{k}}}{2})
i.e.,
{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}=\mathbb{S}_{\nicefrac{{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}}{{2}}}^{\Lambda}\cap\mathbb{S}_{-\nicefrac{{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}}{{2}}}^{\Lambda}
. This new integration region is mirror-symmetric with respect to all axis, allowing for a simpler parametrization of the integration bounds.

Figure 17
:

Region of integration in the 3-momentum sphere intersection regularization scheme used to regularize the
\mathcal{A}
integral.

In Fig.
17
we show the 2-dimensional cross section of the intersection
{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}
, in the
p_{y}-p_{z}
plane. Consider the more general case of an integral,
I
, with an integrand,
F
, which is independent of the azimuthal angle,
\phi
:

\displaystyle I=\int_{{\mathbb{I}^{\prime}}_{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}^{\Lambda}}\differential[3]{\vec{p}}F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta].

(230)

This integral can be separated into two parts: one corresponding to the upper hemisphere and the other to the lower hemisphere of the intersection. Consider also the functions
B_{U}
and
B_{L}
which parametrize the boundary of the upper and lower hemispheres, respectively (see Fig.
17
). Hence, we can write,
I=I_{U}+I_{L}
, where:

\displaystyle\frac{I_{U}}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{2}}}\differential{\theta}\sin[\theta]F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta]-\int_{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{0}^{B_{U}}\differential{\theta}\sin[\theta]F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta],

(231)

\displaystyle\frac{I_{L}}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{2}}}\differential{\theta}\sin[\theta]F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta]-\int_{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{B_{L}}^{\pi}\differential{\theta}\sin[\theta]F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta].

(232)

In the above, we first consider the integral of a sphere of radius
p_{\mathrm{Max}}
(the distance from the origin to the point where both boundaries intersect,
B_{U}=B_{L}
, which occurs when
\theta=\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{2}}
) and than subtract the extra volume coming from the boundaries,
B_{U}
and
B_{L}
, up to this maximum sphere. The quantity
p_{\mathrm{Min}}
is the distance from the origin to the boundaries when
\theta=0
. The quantities
B_{U}
,
B_{L}
,
p_{\mathrm{Min}}
and
p_{\mathrm{Max}}
, will be defined later. For simplicity, we apply the change of variables,
\alpha=\cos[\theta]
and change the integration bounds accordingly to get:

\displaystyle-\frac{I}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{1}^{-1}\differential{\alpha}F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\alpha]

\displaystyle-\int_{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{1}^{B_{U}}\differential{\alpha}F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\alpha]

\displaystyle-\int_{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{B_{L}}^{-1}\differential{\alpha}F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\alpha].

(233)

This expression can be further simplified by breaking the
\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}
integral in the first term at the point
p_{\mathrm{Min}}
and by rearranging the integral. Finally, we can write the integral as:

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=-2\pi\quantity(
\int_0^{ p_{\mathrm{Min}} } \dd{\abs{\vec{p}}}
\vec{p}^2
\int_{ 1 }^{ -1 } \dd{\alpha}
F [\abs{\vec{p}},\alpha]
+
\int_{ p_{\mathrm{Min}} }^{ p_{\mathrm{Max}} } \dd{\abs{\vec{p}}}
\vec{p}^2
\int_{ B_U }^{ B_L } \dd{\alpha}
F [\abs{\vec{p}},\alpha]
).

(234)

At this point we define the functions that define the integration bounds. For the different hemispheres, the equations which define the boundaries are given by:

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{2}\sin[\theta]^{2}\cos[\phi]^{2}+\bm{p}^{2}\sin[\theta]^{2}\sin[\phi]^{2}+\quantity( \abs{\vec{p}} \cos[\theta] \pm\frac{\abs{\vec{k}}}{2} )^{2}=\Lambda^{2}.

(235)

Where the plus sign is for the upper hemisphere and the minus sign for the lower hemisphere. Solving these equations with respect to
\cos[\theta]
, yields:

\displaystyle\cos[\theta]=\pm\frac{\Lambda^{2}-\nicefrac{{\bm{k}^{2}}}{{4}}-\bm{p}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}}.

(236)

Again, the plus sign is for the upper region while the minus sign is for the lower hemisphere. Thus, in the
\alpha=\cos[\theta]
variable we can find write
B_{U}
and
B_{L}
as:

\displaystyle B_{U}\quantity[\Lambda, \abs{\vec{k}}, \abs{\vec{p}}]=\frac{\Lambda^{2}-\nicefrac{{\bm{k}^{2}}}{{4}}-\bm{p}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}}=-B_{L}\quantity[\Lambda, \abs{\vec{k}}, \abs{\vec{p}}].

(237)

The quantities
p_{\mathrm{Min}}
and
p_{\mathrm{Max}}
can be derived by plugging
\theta=0
and
\theta=\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{2}}
in Eq. (
236
), to yield:

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{Min}}\quantity[\Lambda, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\Lambda-\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2},

(238)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{Max}}\quantity[\Lambda, \abs{\vec{k}}]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}}.

(239)

We are finally ready to evaluate the integral
R_{ij}
. Applying the tools developed above, we can write
F[\absolutevalue{\vec{p}},\theta]=1/\quantity(
E\qty[M_i, \vec{p} + \frac{\vec{k}}{2}]
E[M_j,\vec{p} - \frac{\vec{k}}{2} ]
)
. Hence, we can explicitly write:

\displaystyle R_{ij}\quantity[\Lambda,M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]=

\displaystyle-\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\int_{0}^{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{1}^{-1}\frac{\differential{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}+\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}-\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{j}^{2}}}

\displaystyle-\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\int_{p_{\mathrm{Min}}}^{p_{\mathrm{Max}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\bm{p}^{2}\int_{B_{U}}^{B_{L}}\frac{\differential{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}+\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}-\bm{p}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{j}^{2}}}.

(240)

We can calculate the innermost integrals analytically:

\displaystyle\int\differential{\alpha}

\displaystyle\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\bm{p}^{2}}{\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}+\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}-\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}\cos[\theta]+M_{j}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=-2\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\arcsin\quantity[
\frac{
\sqrt{
\frac{\vec{k}^2}{4} + M_j^2 + \vec{p}^2 - \alpha\abs{\vec{k}} \abs{\vec{p}} }
}{
\sqrt{2}
\sqrt{
\frac{\vec{k}^2}{4} +
\frac{ M_i^2 + M_j^2 }{2} + \vec{p}^2 }
}
]+\mathrm{cte}.

(241)

Using this primitive, we are finally able to write
R_{ij}
as:

\displaystyle R_{ij}\quantity[\Lambda,M_i, M_j, \abs{\vec{k}} ]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\eta\int_{0}^{\Lambda-\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\arcsin\quantity[
\frac{
\sqrt{
\frac{\vec{k}^2}{4} + M_j^2 + \vec{p}^2 + \eta\abs{\vec{k}} \abs{\vec{p}} }
}{
\sqrt{2}
\sqrt{
\frac{\vec{k}^2}{4} +
\frac{ M_i^2 + M_j^2 }{2} + \vec{p}^2 }
}
]

\displaystyle+2\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\eta\int_{\Lambda-\frac{\absolutevalue{\vec{k}}}{2}}^{\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{4}}}\differential{\abs{\vec{p}}}\absolutevalue{\vec{p}}\arcsin\quantity[
\frac{
\sqrt{
M_j^2 + \eta\Lambda^2 + (1-\eta)(\vec{p}^2 + \frac{\vec{k}^2}{4}) }
}{
\sqrt{2}
\sqrt{
\frac{\vec{k}^2}{4} +
\frac{ M_i^2 + M_j^2 }{2} + \vec{p}^2 }
}
].

(242)

In Table
9
we give some results from solving the integral which can then be directly compared to the results obtained when using the variables
E
and
\varepsilon
, given in Figs.

and

.

), for different values of the cutoff,

, effective masses,

. The

integral is identical to the area defined by the integral
\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{ \varepsilon}\int_{E_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}^{E_{\mathrm{max}}^{(\differential{\varepsilon}\differential{E})}}\differential{E}
and

) and Figs.
